Q: A 58-year-old man develops a painful vesicular rash confined to the T5 dermatome (herpes zoster). The virus lies dormant in the cell bodies of sensory neurons. In which of the following structures are the cell bodies of these unipolar (pseudounipolar) neurons located?
A) Anterior horn of the spinal cord
B) Lateral horn of the spinal cord
C) Sympathetic chain ganglion
D) Dorsal root ganglion
E) Posterior horn of the spinal cord
Answer: D
Explanation: Unipolar (pseudounipolar) neurons are the sensory neurons of the PNS and are found in the dorsal root ganglia and cranial nerve ganglia. The anterior horn contains multipolar somatic motor neurons, the lateral horn (T1–L2) contains preganglionic sympathetic neurons, sympathetic chain ganglia contain multipolar postganglionic neurons, and the posterior horn contains interneurons on which the central processes of sensory neurons synapse.

Q: A 26-year-old motorcyclist has a spinal cord lesion that selectively destroys the lateral horn between the T1 and L2 segments. Which of the following neuron populations is most likely lost?
A) Preganglionic parasympathetic neurons
B) Postganglionic sympathetic neurons
C) General somatic efferent neurons
D) Pseudounipolar sensory neurons
E) Preganglionic sympathetic neurons
Answer: E
Explanation: The lateral horn of the T1–L2 spinal cord segments contains the cell bodies of preganglionic sympathetic (general visceral efferent) neurons. Postganglionic sympathetic cell bodies lie in the sympathetic chain or prevertebral ganglia, preganglionic parasympathetic cell bodies lie in brainstem nuclei and S2–S4, somatic motor neurons lie in the anterior horn, and pseudounipolar neurons lie in the dorsal root ganglia.

Q: A patient has a brainstem lesion that interrupts all cranial preganglionic parasympathetic outflow. Which set of cranial nerves carries these fibers?
A) Trigeminal, facial, vagus, and accessory
B) Oculomotor, facial, glossopharyngeal, and vagus
C) Oculomotor, trochlear, abducens, and hypoglossal
D) Facial, vestibulocochlear, glossopharyngeal, and accessory
E) Olfactory, optic, trigeminal, and vagus
Answer: B
Explanation: The craniosacral (parasympathetic) outflow travels in cranial nerves III, VII, IX, and X and in the pelvic splanchnic nerves (S2–S4). The other combinations include nerves (trigeminal, trochlear, abducens, accessory, hypoglossal, olfactory, optic, vestibulocochlear) that carry no preganglionic parasympathetic fibers.

Q: A 30-year-old woman with multiple sclerosis has patchy demyelination of axons in the brain and spinal cord. Which of the following cells forms the myelin that is being destroyed?
A) Schwann cell
B) Astrocyte
C) Microglial cell
D) Oligodendrocyte
E) Satellite cell
Answer: D
Explanation: Myelin in the CNS is formed by oligodendrocytes, and one oligodendrocyte can myelinate several axons. Schwann cells form myelin in the PNS, satellite cells support ganglion cell bodies, astrocytes are supportive glia, and microglia phagocytose waste products of nerve tissue.

Q: During thoracic surgery, a surgeon injures the thoracic duct, producing a milky pleural effusion. Lymph from which of the following regions would still drain normally through a different channel?
A) Left upper limb
B) Right upper limb
C) Left side of the head and neck
D) Both lower limbs
E) Abdominal viscera
Answer: B
Explanation: The thoracic duct drains lymph from the entire body except the right upper quadrant (right side of the head and neck, right upper limb, and right thorax), which drains through the right lymphatic duct. Lymph from the left upper limb, left head and neck, lower limbs, and abdominal viscera all enters the thoracic duct.

Q: A 62-year-old woman with osteoarthritis has pain at the base of her thumb and difficulty with opposition. Which type of synovial joint is the first carpometacarpal joint?
A) Hinge joint
B) Pivot joint
C) Saddle joint
D) Plane joint
E) Ball-and-socket joint
Answer: C
Explanation: The first carpometacarpal joint is a saddle joint, which permits flexion, extension, abduction, adduction, and circumduction, and is the basis for opposition of the thumb. Hinge joints (elbow) allow flexion and extension only, pivot joints (atlantoaxial) allow rotation, and plane joints allow gliding.

Q: A 20-year-old soccer player is struck on the front of the knee. Radiographs show a fracture of a bone that lies within the tendon of the quadriceps femoris. How is this bone classified?
A) Sesamoid bone
B) Long bone
C) Short bone
D) Flat bone
E) Irregular bone
Answer: A
Explanation: The patella is a sesamoid bone, which develops within a tendon (here, the quadriceps tendon) and protects the tendon and improves its mechanical advantage. Long bones (femur), short bones (carpals), flat bones (scapula), and irregular bones (vertebrae) are classified by shape rather than by their relationship to a tendon.

Q: A 35-year-old woman with neck pain has an MRI that shows compression of a spinal nerve as it leaves the vertebral canal between the seventh cervical and first thoracic vertebrae. Which spinal nerve is compressed?
A) C7
B) T1
C) C8
D) C6
E) T2
Answer: C
Explanation: There are eight cervical spinal nerves but only seven cervical vertebrae. C1–C7 exit above their corresponding vertebrae, whereas C8 exits between C7 and T1. From T1 downward, each spinal nerve exits below its corresponding vertebra.

Q: A patient undergoes a total sympathectomy for hyperhidrosis. Which of the following structures is innervated exclusively by sympathetic fibers and would lose its autonomic supply?
A) Ciliary muscle
B) Lacrimal gland
C) Smooth muscle of the gastrointestinal tract
D) Sweat glands of the skin
E) Cardiac muscle
Answer: D
Explanation: Sweat glands, arrector pili muscles, and the smooth muscle of blood vessels receive only sympathetic innervation. The ciliary muscle and lacrimal gland are supplied by parasympathetic fibers, and the gastrointestinal smooth muscle and heart receive both parasympathetic and sympathetic innervation.

Q: A 45-year-old man with chronic pancreatitis develops steatorrhea and later diabetes mellitus. Which feature of the pancreas explains why it can produce both digestive enzymes and hormones?
A) It is a purely endocrine gland like the suprarenal gland
B) It is a purely exocrine gland that secretes through a single duct
C) It is an intraperitoneal organ with a long mesentery
D) It receives parasympathetic innervation only
E) It has both exocrine acini and endocrine islets of Langerhans
Answer: E
Explanation: The pancreas is a mixed gland: its exocrine acinar cells secrete digestive enzymes into the pancreatic duct, while the islets of Langerhans secrete insulin and glucagon directly into the blood. The suprarenal gland is a purely endocrine organ, and the pancreas is largely retroperitoneal rather than intraperitoneal.

Q: A 25-year-old baseball player falls on his hand and has point tenderness at the base of the hypothenar eminence. Radiographs show a fracture of the hook of the hamate. Which structure is most at risk of injury?
A) Median nerve
B) Ulnar nerve
C) Radial artery
D) Superficial branch of the radial nerve
E) Tendon of the flexor pollicis longus
Answer: B
Explanation: The hook of the hamate forms part of the ulnar (Guyon) canal, so its fracture can injure the ulnar nerve and artery, producing weakness of the intrinsic hand muscles and sensory loss in the ring and little fingers. The median nerve and flexor pollicis longus lie deep to the flexor retinaculum, and the radial structures are on the lateral side of the wrist.

Q: A 20-year-old man fractures the surgical neck of his humerus in a fall. Which of the following sensory findings is most likely?
A) Loss of sensation over the dorsum of the hand
B) Loss of sensation over the skin of the lateral shoulder (deltoid region)
C) Loss of sensation over the medial side of the forearm
D) Loss of sensation over the tip of the thumb
E) Loss of sensation over the medial side of the arm
Answer: B
Explanation: The axillary nerve winds around the surgical neck of the humerus and gives rise to the superior lateral cutaneous nerve of the arm, which supplies the skin over the lower deltoid. Its injury also paralyzes the deltoid and teres minor (weak abduction and lateral rotation). Hand, thumb, and medial forearm sensation depend on the radial, median, and medial antebrachial cutaneous nerves respectively.

Q: A 22-year-old rugby player has a recurrent shoulder dislocation. The humeral head usually dislocates in an anteroinferior direction. Which of the following best explains this?
A) The coracoacromial arch prevents anterior displacement of the head
B) The inferior part of the capsule lacks support from rotator cuff tendons
C) The glenoid cavity is deeper inferiorly than superiorly
D) The supraspinatus tendon reinforces the inferior capsule
E) The long head of the triceps fuses with the superior capsule
Answer: B
Explanation: The glenohumeral joint is very mobile but relatively unstable, and the inferior capsule is not reinforced by rotator cuff tendons, so dislocation is typically anteroinferior and may injure the axillary nerve. The supraspinatus reinforces the superior capsule, and the coracoacromial arch prevents upward displacement.

Q: A 55-year-old painter has pain with overhead work and cannot initiate abduction of his arm. An MRI shows a tear in the most commonly injured rotator cuff tendon. Which muscle is involved?
A) Infraspinatus
B) Teres minor
C) Supraspinatus
D) Subscapularis
E) Deltoid
Answer: C
Explanation: The supraspinatus initiates the first 15 degrees of abduction and is the rotator cuff muscle most commonly torn because its tendon is compressed beneath the acromion. The rotator cuff comprises the supraspinatus, infraspinatus, teres minor, and subscapularis; the deltoid is not part of the cuff and abducts beyond the initial range.

Q: A 48-year-old woman has a radical mastectomy with axillary node dissection. Postoperatively, the medial border of her scapula protrudes when she pushes against a wall. Which nerve was injured?
A) Long thoracic nerve
B) Thoracodorsal nerve
C) Dorsal scapular nerve
D) Medial pectoral nerve
E) Suprascapular nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The long thoracic nerve (C5–C7) runs on the superficial surface of the serratus anterior; its injury causes a winged scapula and difficulty raising the arm above horizontal. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies the latissimus dorsi, the dorsal scapular nerve supplies the rhomboids, the medial pectoral nerve supplies the pectoralis minor and major, and the suprascapular nerve supplies the supraspinatus and infraspinatus.

Q: A 66-year-old smoker has weakness of the small muscles of the hand, numbness along the medial forearm, and ipsilateral ptosis and miosis. Chest radiography shows an apical lung tumor. Which part of the brachial plexus is most likely involved?
A) Upper trunk (C5–C6)
B) Middle trunk (C7)
C) Lateral cord
D) Lower trunk (C8–T1)
E) Posterior cord
Answer: D
Explanation: A Pancoast (apical lung) tumor compresses the lower trunk (C8–T1) and the adjacent sympathetic chain, producing weakness of intrinsic hand muscles, medial forearm sensory loss, and Horner syndrome. Upper trunk lesions (Erb palsy) cause a waiter's tip posture, and cord lesions give deficits in specific nerve territories.

Q: Which of the following arteries is a branch of the subscapular artery and takes part in the anastomosis with the suprascapular artery around the scapula?
A) Thoracodorsal artery
B) Lateral thoracic artery
C) Posterior circumflex humeral artery
D) Deep brachial artery
E) Circumflex scapular artery
Answer: E
Explanation: The circumflex scapular artery, a branch of the subscapular artery, passes through the triangular space and anastomoses with the suprascapular and dorsal scapular arteries, providing collateral flow if the axillary artery is ligated. The thoracodorsal artery supplies the latissimus dorsi, the lateral thoracic artery supplies the breast and serratus anterior, and the other vessels are not part of this anastomosis.

Q: A 52-year-old woman has a carcinoma in the upper outer quadrant of her breast. To which group of lymph nodes does most of this lymph drain first?
A) Pectoral (anterior) axillary nodes
B) Parasternal nodes
C) Posterior (subscapular) axillary nodes
D) Humeral (lateral) axillary nodes
E) Apical axillary nodes
Answer: A
Explanation: Most of the lymph from the breast (about 75%), especially from the lateral quadrants, drains to the pectoral (anterior) axillary nodes and then to the central and apical nodes. The parasternal nodes drain the medial breast, the subscapular nodes drain the posterior thoracic wall, and the humeral nodes drain the upper limb.

Q: A 56-year-old woman has a firm breast mass with skin dimpling and nipple retraction. Which structure is most likely infiltrated and shortened by the tumor?
A) Lactiferous sinuses
B) Serratus anterior muscle
C) Retromammary space
D) Pectoralis minor muscle
E) Suspensory ligaments of Cooper
Answer: E
Explanation: The suspensory (Cooper) ligaments run from the dermis to the deep layer of the superficial fascia through the breast; tumor invasion shortens them and dimples the skin. Advanced inflammatory carcinoma produces peau d'orange. The retromammary space allows mobility over the pectoral fascia.

Q: A 30-year-old man has a midshaft humerus fracture and develops wrist drop with sensory loss over the dorsum of the lateral hand. Which of the following movements is most likely preserved?
A) Extension of the wrist
B) Extension of the forearm at the elbow
C) Extension of the metacarpophalangeal joints
D) Abduction of the thumb
E) Supination of the forearm with the elbow extended
Answer: B
Explanation: The radial nerve lies in the radial (spiral) groove on the posterior humerus, and a midshaft fracture damages it distal to the origin of its branches to the triceps, so elbow extension is preserved. Wrist extension, finger extension, thumb abduction, and supination are all supplied by branches arising at or below the groove.

Q: A 40-year-old data-entry clerk has nocturnal numbness and tingling of the palmar surface of the lateral three and one-half digits and weak thumb opposition. Which area is most likely spared?
A) Palmar skin of the index finger
B) Palmar skin of the thumb
C) Palmar skin of the middle finger
D) Palmar skin of the lateral half of the ring finger
E) Skin over the thenar eminence
Answer: E
Explanation: In carpal tunnel syndrome the median nerve is compressed beneath the flexor retinaculum. The palmar cutaneous branch arises proximal to the retinaculum and passes superficial to it, so sensation over the thenar eminence is spared. The digits supplied by the median nerve are affected, and the thenar muscles may eventually waste.

Q: A 35-year-old man fractures his medial epicondyle and has difficulty holding a sheet of paper between his thumb and index finger. Which muscle is most likely paralyzed?
A) Adductor pollicis
B) Opponens pollicis
C) Abductor pollicis brevis
D) Flexor pollicis longus
E) First lumbrical
Answer: A
Explanation: The adductor pollicis is innervated by the deep branch of the ulnar nerve, which runs behind the medial epicondyle and is vulnerable there. The opponens pollicis and abductor pollicis brevis are supplied by the recurrent branch of the median nerve, the flexor pollicis longus by the anterior interosseous nerve, and the first lumbrical by the median nerve.

Q: A 50-year-old man has a radial head dislocation and cannot extend his fingers at the metacarpophalangeal joints, although wrist extension is present and there is no sensory loss. Which nerve is most likely injured?
A) Superficial branch of the radial nerve
B) Median nerve
C) Ulnar nerve
D) Deep branch of the radial nerve (posterior interosseous nerve)
E) Musculocutaneous nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The deep branch of the radial nerve winds around the radial neck through the supinator and supplies the finger and thumb extensors; being purely motor, it causes no sensory loss. Wrist extension is preserved because the extensor carpi radialis longus is supplied by the radial nerve before it divides.

Q: A phlebotomist inserts a needle into the median cubital vein. Which structure lies between this vein and the brachial artery and median nerve and protects them?
A) Brachioradialis muscle
B) Bicipital aponeurosis
C) Pronator teres muscle
D) Brachialis tendon
E) Flexor carpi radialis tendon
Answer: B
Explanation: The bicipital aponeurosis, an expansion of the biceps tendon, forms part of the roof of the cubital fossa and lies deep to the median cubital vein, covering the brachial artery and median nerve. The cubital fossa contains, from lateral to medial, the radial nerve, biceps tendon, brachial artery, and median nerve.

Q: A 19-year-old man falls on his outstretched hand and has tenderness in the anatomic snuffbox. The medial (ulnar) boundary of the snuffbox is formed by the tendon of which muscle?
A) Extensor pollicis brevis
B) Abductor pollicis longus
C) Extensor carpi radialis longus
D) Extensor pollicis longus
E) Extensor indicis
Answer: D
Explanation: The anatomic snuffbox is bounded medially by the extensor pollicis longus tendon and laterally by the tendons of the abductor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis. The scaphoid and trapezium form its floor, and the radial artery crosses it, so snuffbox tenderness suggests a scaphoid fracture.

Q: A cardiologist implants a pacemaker lead through a vein that ascends in the deltopectoral groove. This vein enters the axillary vein after piercing which structure?
A) Pectoralis major muscle
B) Clavipectoral fascia
C) Subclavius muscle
D) Coracobrachialis muscle
E) Axillary sheath
Answer: B
Explanation: The cephalic vein runs in the deltopectoral groove, pierces the clavipectoral fascia, and drains into the axillary vein. It is a common site for venous access. The clavipectoral fascia is also pierced by the lateral pectoral nerve and the thoracoacromial vessels.

Q: A 28-year-old chef lacerates the base of his thenar eminence. He cannot oppose his thumb but can adduct it, and sensation is normal. Which nerve branch was cut?
A) Deep branch of the ulnar nerve
B) Palmar digital branch of the median nerve
C) Recurrent branch of the median nerve
D) Superficial branch of the radial nerve
E) Palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The recurrent (thenar) branch of the median nerve supplies the abductor pollicis brevis, flexor pollicis brevis (superficial head), and opponens pollicis, so injury impairs opposition without sensory loss. Adduction depends on the adductor pollicis (ulnar nerve), and the palmar cutaneous branch is purely sensory.

Q: A volleyball player has weakness in initiating abduction and in lateral rotation of the shoulder, with atrophy of the supraspinatus and infraspinatus. Where is the nerve most likely entrapped?
A) Suprascapular notch, beneath the superior transverse scapular ligament
B) Quadrangular space
C) Triangular space
D) Radial groove of the humerus
E) Cubital tunnel
Answer: A
Explanation: The suprascapular nerve passes through the suprascapular notch beneath the superior transverse scapular ligament, whereas the suprascapular artery passes above it. Entrapment affects both the supraspinatus and infraspinatus. The quadrangular space transmits the axillary nerve, and the radial groove transmits the radial nerve.

Q: A 20-year-old man has a stab wound near the surgical neck of the humerus. Which structure normally passes through the quadrangular space?
A) Radial nerve
B) Circumflex scapular artery
C) Suprascapular nerve
D) Axillary nerve
E) Deep brachial artery
Answer: D
Explanation: The quadrangular space is bounded by the teres minor, teres major, long head of the triceps, and surgical neck of the humerus, and it transmits the axillary nerve and posterior circumflex humeral vessels. The circumflex scapular artery passes through the triangular space, and the radial nerve and deep brachial artery pass through the triangular interval.

Q: A clinician performs an Allen test on a 30-year-old patient. After compression of both the radial and ulnar arteries, release of the ulnar artery causes the entire hand to flush. Which structure supplies this collateral flow, being formed mainly from the ulnar artery?
A) Deep palmar arch
B) Princeps pollicis artery
C) Anterior interosseous artery
D) Superficial palmar arch
E) Dorsal carpal arch
Answer: D
Explanation: The superficial palmar arch is the direct continuation of the ulnar artery and is completed by the superficial palmar branch of the radial artery. The deep palmar arch is formed chiefly by the radial artery and completed by the deep branch of the ulnar artery. A normal Allen test demonstrates communication between the two systems.

Q: A 22-year-old man lacerates his wrist just proximal to the pisiform. The ring and little fingers assume a claw posture. Which muscles are most directly responsible for this deformity when paralyzed?
A) Lateral two lumbricals
B) Flexor digitorum superficialis
C) Flexor digitorum profundus to the little finger
D) Medial two lumbricals
E) Extensor digitorum
Answer: D
Explanation: The ulnar nerve supplies the medial two lumbricals and the interossei; their paralysis allows unopposed extensor digitorum to hyperextend the MCP joints while the long flexors flex the IP joints (claw hand). The lateral two lumbricals are supplied by the median nerve and are spared.

Q: During axillary surgery, a surgeon describes the segment of the axillary artery that lies deep to the pectoralis minor. Which part of the artery is this?
A) First part
B) Second part
C) Third part
D) First and second parts together
E) Third part and the beginning of the brachial artery
Answer: B
Explanation: The pectoralis minor crosses the axillary artery and divides it into three parts: the first part is proximal (medial) to the muscle, the second part is deep to it, and the third part is distal (lateral) to it. The thoracoacromial and lateral thoracic arteries arise from the second part.

Q: A 32-year-old carpenter has a puncture wound at the base of his thumb followed by swelling and pain in the flexor sheath of the thumb. Infection in this sheath can spread through which structure to the forearm?
A) Radial bursa (sheath of the flexor pollicis longus)
B) Common flexor sheath (ulnar bursa) directly
C) Midpalmar space
D) Thenar space
E) Dorsal subcutaneous space
Answer: A
Explanation: The synovial sheath of the flexor pollicis longus tendon is continuous with the radial bursa, so thumb tenosynovitis can spread through the carpal tunnel to the forearm. The sheath of the little finger is usually continuous with the common flexor sheath (ulnar bursa). The fascial spaces of the palm (thenar and midpalmar) are separate from the synovial sheaths.

Q: A 60-year-old man has a progressive flexion contracture of the ring and little fingers with nodular thickening in the palm. Which structure is primarily involved?
A) Palmar aponeurosis
B) Flexor retinaculum
C) Extensor expansion
D) Flexor digitorum profundus tendon
E) Ulnar nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: Dupuytren contracture is a progressive thickening, shortening, and fibrosis of the palmar fascia, especially the palmar aponeurosis, pulling the fingers toward the palm. The flexor retinaculum is compressed in carpal tunnel syndrome, and the tendons and nerve are not primarily diseased in this condition.

Q: A 78-year-old woman falls and has a displaced fracture of the femoral neck. She is at high risk of avascular necrosis of the femoral head. Which artery normally provides the main blood supply to the femoral head and neck in an adult?
A) Lateral circumflex femoral artery
B) Obturator artery
C) Medial circumflex femoral artery
D) Superior gluteal artery
E) Inferior epigastric artery
Answer: C
Explanation: The retinacular branches of the medial circumflex femoral artery (a branch of the deep femoral artery) provide most of the blood to the femoral head and neck in adults, so a displaced femoral neck fracture disrupts this supply. The artery of the ligament of the head (from the obturator artery) is small and inadequate.

Q: A 28-year-old driver strikes the dashboard with his knee in a collision. The hip is flexed, adducted, and medially rotated, with a palpable mass in the buttock. Which nerve is most at risk of injury?
A) Femoral nerve
B) Obturator nerve
C) Sciatic nerve
D) Superior gluteal nerve
E) Pudendal nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: Posterior dislocation of the hip displaces the femoral head posterior to the acetabulum, where the sciatic nerve passes inferior to the piriformis, so it may be stretched or compressed. The femoral and obturator nerves lie anteriorly and medially and are unlikely to be injured.

Q: A 35-year-old woman received an intramuscular injection in the gluteal region. When she stands on her right leg, the left side of her pelvis sags. Which nerve was most likely injured?
A) Left superior gluteal nerve
B) Right inferior gluteal nerve
C) Right sciatic nerve
D) Right obturator nerve
E) Right superior gluteal nerve
Answer: E
Explanation: The superior gluteal nerve supplies the gluteus medius and minimus, which abduct the thigh and stabilize the pelvis during single-leg stance. When these muscles are paralyzed (positive Trendelenburg sign), the pelvis drops on the unsupported side, the side opposite the lesion. Injections should be given in the upper outer quadrant to avoid the sciatic nerve.

Q: A 60-year-old man after hip surgery cannot rise from a seated position or climb stairs, and extension of the thigh against resistance is weak. Which nerve was most likely injured?
A) Superior gluteal nerve
B) Femoral nerve
C) Nerve to the obturator internus
D) Tibial nerve
E) Inferior gluteal nerve
Answer: E
Explanation: The inferior gluteal nerve supplies the gluteus maximus, the main extensor of the thigh, which is used in rising from sitting and climbing stairs. The superior gluteal nerve supplies the gluteus medius and minimus, and the femoral nerve supplies the quadriceps.

Q: During cardiac catheterization through the femoral vein, the physician must avoid the femoral artery. Which structure lies immediately medial to the femoral artery within the femoral sheath?
A) Femoral nerve
B) Great saphenous vein
C) Femoral vein
D) Obturator nerve
E) Pectineus muscle
Answer: C
Explanation: From lateral to medial, the femoral triangle contains the femoral nerve, artery, vein, and lymphatics (the nerve lies outside the femoral sheath). The femoral vein is thus immediately medial to the artery, and the femoral canal medial to the vein contains lymphatics and fat.

Q: A 65-year-old woman has a tender bulge below and lateral to the pubic tubercle that proves to be a femoral hernia. Which structure forms the medial boundary of the femoral ring through which the hernia enters?
A) Femoral vein
B) Inguinal ligament
C) Lacunar ligament
D) Pectineal ligament
E) Iliopsoas muscle
Answer: C
Explanation: The femoral ring is bounded anteriorly by the inguinal ligament, posteriorly by the pectineal ligament, laterally by the femoral vein, and medially by the lacunar ligament. Femoral hernias are more common in women and are prone to strangulation because of the rigid medial border.

Q: A 45-year-old man has numbness on the medial side of his leg and foot after knee surgery. Which nerve accompanies the femoral vessels in the adductor canal and then continues down the leg with the great saphenous vein?
A) Obturator nerve
B) Sural nerve
C) Saphenous nerve
D) Lateral femoral cutaneous nerve
E) Superficial peroneal nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The saphenous nerve is the longest branch of the femoral nerve; it passes through the adductor canal, emerges between the sartorius and gracilis, and supplies the skin of the medial leg and foot, accompanying the great saphenous vein. The sural and superficial peroneal nerves supply the lateral and dorsal foot, and the lateral femoral cutaneous nerve supplies the lateral thigh.

Q: A 55-year-old anticoagulated man has a large hematoma in the psoas major muscle. He has weak knee extension, an absent patellar reflex, and numbness over the anterior thigh and medial leg. Which nerve is compressed?
A) Obturator nerve
B) Lateral femoral cutaneous nerve
C) Genitofemoral nerve
D) Femoral nerve
E) Sciatic nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The femoral nerve (L2–L4) lies in the groove between the psoas major and iliacus, supplies the quadriceps femoris (knee extension and patellar reflex), and gives anterior femoral cutaneous and saphenous branches. The obturator nerve supplies the adductors, and the sciatic nerve supplies the posterior thigh and leg.

Q: During pelvic surgery near the ovarian fossa, a 38-year-old woman sustains a nerve injury and later has weakness in adducting the thigh with numbness over the medial thigh. Which nerve was injured?
A) Obturator nerve
B) Femoral nerve
C) Genitofemoral nerve
D) Ilioinguinal nerve
E) Pudendal nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The obturator nerve (L2–L4) runs along the lateral pelvic wall, crosses the ovarian fossa, passes through the obturator canal, and supplies the adductor muscles and the skin of the medial thigh. The femoral nerve supplies the anterior thigh, and the pudendal nerve supplies the perineum.

Q: A 30-year-old man has a fracture of the neck of the fibula. Which of the following functions is most likely lost?
A) Plantar flexion and inversion of the foot
B) Flexion of the knee
C) Extension of the hip
D) Flexion of the toes
E) Dorsiflexion and eversion of the foot
Answer: E
Explanation: The common peroneal (fibular) nerve winds around the neck of the fibula and divides into the deep peroneal nerve (dorsiflexors) and superficial peroneal nerve (evertors). Its injury produces foot drop, a steppage gait, and sensory loss on the dorsum of the foot. Plantar flexion, inversion, toe flexion, and knee flexion are supplied by the tibial nerve and other branches of the sciatic nerve.

Q: A 40-year-old man has a deep laceration in the popliteal fossa and can no longer plantar flex or invert his foot. He has sensory loss over the sole. Which nerve was most likely transected?
A) Common peroneal nerve
B) Deep peroneal nerve
C) Tibial nerve
D) Superficial peroneal nerve
E) Sural nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The tibial nerve supplies the posterior compartment of the leg (plantar flexors and invertors) and, through the medial and lateral plantar nerves, the sole of the foot. The peroneal nerves supply dorsiflexors and evertors, and the sural nerve is purely sensory to the lateral foot.

Q: A 24-year-old driver strikes the dashboard with the proximal tibia. On examination, the tibia can be displaced posteriorly beneath the femur (positive posterior drawer sign). Which ligament is most likely torn?
A) Anterior cruciate ligament
B) Tibial collateral ligament
C) Fibular collateral ligament
D) Patellar ligament
E) Posterior cruciate ligament
Answer: E
Explanation: The posterior cruciate ligament prevents posterior displacement of the tibia relative to the femur (and anterior displacement of the femur on the tibia). The anterior cruciate prevents anterior displacement of the tibia (anterior drawer sign), and the collateral ligaments resist valgus and varus stress.

Q: A 20-year-old man has a supracondylar fracture of the femur and diminished pedal pulses. Which structure is the deepest in the popliteal fossa and most likely injured?
A) Popliteal vein
B) Tibial nerve
C) Common peroneal nerve
D) Popliteal artery
E) Small saphenous vein
Answer: D
Explanation: From superficial to deep, the main structures in the popliteal fossa are the tibial nerve, popliteal vein, and popliteal artery, so the artery lies closest to the femur and is vulnerable in supracondylar fractures. The common peroneal nerve runs along the medial border of the biceps femoris, and the small saphenous vein enters from the superficial fascia.

Q: A surgeon performs a venous cutdown on the great saphenous vein just anterior to the medial malleolus. Which nerve accompanies the vein at this site and is at risk?
A) Sural nerve
B) Saphenous nerve
C) Superficial peroneal nerve
D) Deep peroneal nerve
E) Tibial nerve
Answer: B
Explanation: The great saphenous vein passes anterior to the medial malleolus accompanied by the saphenous nerve, which may be included in a ligature during cutdown. The sural nerve accompanies the small saphenous vein behind the lateral malleolus.

Q: A 50-year-old woman has varicose veins of the leg. Which veins normally direct blood from the superficial to the deep venous system through valves?
A) Small saphenous veins
B) Popliteal veins
C) Perforating veins
D) Femoral veins
E) Anterior tibial veins
Answer: C
Explanation: Perforating veins pierce the deep fascia and carry blood from the superficial (saphenous) veins to the deep veins, and their valves prevent reflux. Incompetent perforating veins allow high-pressure deep venous blood to enter the superficial system, causing varicosities.

Q: A 24-year-old marathon runner has severe pain in the anterior leg, loss of dorsiflexion, and numbness between the first and second toes. Which nerve is compressed within the anterior compartment?
A) Deep peroneal nerve
B) Superficial peroneal nerve
C) Tibial nerve
D) Sural nerve
E) Saphenous nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The deep peroneal nerve and anterior tibial artery lie in the anterior compartment, whose tight fascial boundaries make it vulnerable to compartment syndrome. The nerve supplies the dorsiflexors and the skin of the first interdigital cleft. The superficial peroneal nerve lies in the lateral compartment.

Q: A 40-year-old basketball player feels a snap behind his ankle and can no longer stand on tiptoe. Rupture of the calcaneal (Achilles) tendon impairs the action of which muscles?
A) Tibialis posterior and flexor hallucis longus
B) Peroneus longus and brevis
C) Tibialis anterior and extensor hallucis longus
D) Gastrocnemius and soleus
E) Popliteus and flexor digitorum longus
Answer: D
Explanation: The calcaneal tendon is formed by the gastrocnemius and soleus (with the plantaris tendon medially) and is the main plantar flexor of the ankle. The deep posterior muscles and peroneals are weaker plantar flexors, and the anterior muscles dorsiflex the foot.

Q: A 22-year-old basketball player lands on another player's foot, forcibly inverting the ankle. Which ligament is most likely torn?
A) Deltoid ligament
B) Plantar calcaneonavicular ligament
C) Interosseous membrane
D) Long plantar ligament
E) Anterior talofibular ligament
Answer: E
Explanation: The lateral ligament of the ankle, especially the anterior talofibular component, is weakest and most commonly torn in inversion injuries. The medial (deltoid) ligament is strong and resists eversion, and the spring and long plantar ligaments support the arches of the foot.

Q: A 7-year-old child has flat feet with collapse of the medial longitudinal arch and sagging of the talar head. Which ligament is most important in supporting the head of the talus?
A) Long plantar ligament
B) Short plantar ligament
C) Deltoid ligament
D) Plantar calcaneonavicular (spring) ligament
E) Anterior talofibular ligament
Answer: D
Explanation: The plantar calcaneonavicular (spring) ligament extends from the sustentaculum tali to the navicular and supports the head of the talus, a key element of the medial longitudinal arch. Stretching of this ligament leads to flatfoot (pes planus).

Q: A 45-year-old runner has burning pain in the sole due to compression of the tibial nerve behind the medial malleolus (tarsal tunnel syndrome). Which tendon lies immediately posterior to the medial malleolus within the tunnel?
A) Tibialis posterior
B) Flexor digitorum longus
C) Flexor hallucis longus
D) Peroneus longus
E) Plantaris
Answer: A
Explanation: From anterior to posterior behind the medial malleolus lie the tendons of the tibialis posterior, flexor digitorum longus, then the posterior tibial artery and vein, tibial nerve, and flexor hallucis longus. The peroneal tendons pass behind the lateral malleolus.

Q: A 30-year-old man has an infected ulcer on the lateral side of the heel. Which group of lymph nodes would most likely receive lymph first?
A) Superficial inguinal nodes
B) Deep inguinal nodes
C) Popliteal nodes
D) External iliac nodes
E) Lumbar (para-aortic) nodes
Answer: C
Explanation: Superficial lymphatics from the lateral foot and heel accompany the small saphenous vein and drain to the popliteal nodes, then to the deep inguinal nodes. Most other superficial lymphatics of the lower limb accompany the great saphenous vein and drain to the superficial inguinal nodes.

Q: An 18-year-old man stands in a hyperextended posture. Which ligament of the hip joint is the strongest and most important in limiting hyperextension?
A) Ischiofemoral ligament
B) Pubofemoral ligament
C) Ligament of the head of the femur
D) Transverse acetabular ligament
E) Iliofemoral ligament
Answer: E
Explanation: The iliofemoral ligament (Y ligament of Bigelow) is the strongest ligament of the body, preventing hyperextension and allowing upright posture with little muscular effort. The ischiofemoral ligament is the weakest, the pubofemoral limits abduction, and the ligament of the head carries a small artery.

Q: A physician palpates the dorsalis pedis pulse on the dorsum of the foot. The artery is felt just lateral to which tendon?
A) Tibialis anterior
B) Extensor digitorum longus
C) Extensor hallucis longus
D) Peroneus tertius
E) Tibialis posterior
Answer: C
Explanation: The dorsalis pedis artery, the continuation of the anterior tibial artery, lies on the dorsum of the foot lateral to the extensor hallucis longus tendon and medial to the first tendon of the extensor digitorum longus. A weak or absent pulse suggests peripheral arterial disease.

Q: A 35-year-old sprinter tears a posterior thigh muscle. Which of the following muscles is innervated by the common fibular (peroneal) part of the sciatic nerve rather than the tibial part?
A) Long head of the biceps femoris
B) Semitendinosus
C) Semimembranosus
D) Short head of the biceps femoris
E) Hamstring part of the adductor magnus
Answer: D
Explanation: The sciatic nerve consists of tibial and common peroneal divisions. All hamstring muscles, including the long head of the biceps femoris, are supplied by the tibial division, except the short head of the biceps femoris, which is supplied by the common peroneal division.

Q: A resident is inserting a chest tube for a pneumothorax and is told to pass the tube just above the upper border of a rib. What is the correct order of the intercostal neurovascular bundle from superior to inferior in the costal groove?
A) Vein, artery, nerve
B) Artery, vein, nerve
C) Nerve, artery, vein
D) Nerve, vein, artery
E) Artery, nerve, vein
Answer: A
Explanation: The intercostal vein, artery, and nerve lie in that order from superior to inferior in the costal groove beneath each rib, between the internal and innermost intercostal muscles. Inserting needles or tubes along the superior border of the lower rib avoids this bundle.

Q: A 60-year-old man in the upright position has a pleural effusion. In which pleural space does the fluid first collect?
A) Costomediastinal recess
B) Oblique fissure
C) Pulmonary ligament
D) Costodiaphragmatic recess
E) Cervical pleura
Answer: D
Explanation: The costodiaphragmatic recess is the lowest and deepest part of the pleural cavity, in which pleural fluid collects in the upright position and from which it is aspirated during thoracentesis. The lung does not normally fill this recess, even in deep inspiration.

Q: A 50-year-old man with inflammation of the pleura over the central part of the diaphragm complains of pain in the shoulder. Which nerve mediates this referred pain?
A) Intercostal nerve
B) Vagus nerve
C) Long thoracic nerve
D) Phrenic nerve
E) Suprascapular nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The mediastinal and central diaphragmatic parietal pleura are supplied by the phrenic nerve (C3–C5), so pain is referred to the shoulder dermatomes (C3–C5). The costal and peripheral diaphragmatic pleura are supplied by intercostal nerves, and the visceral pleura is insensitive to pain.

Q: A 2-year-old child aspirates a peanut. Where is it most likely to lodge?
A) Right main bronchus
B) Left main bronchus
C) Trachea above the carina
D) Left superior lobar bronchus
E) Esophagus
Answer: A
Explanation: The right main bronchus is shorter, wider, and more vertical than the left, so aspirated foreign bodies enter it more readily. The left main bronchus is longer and narrower and passes under the aortic arch.

Q: A surgeon plans a segmentectomy for a localized lung lesion. Which structure normally runs between adjacent bronchopulmonary segments and marks the intersegmental plane?
A) Tributaries of the pulmonary vein
B) Segmental bronchus
C) Segmental branch of the pulmonary artery
D) Bronchial artery
E) Pulmonary ligament
Answer: A
Explanation: Each bronchopulmonary segment is supplied by a tertiary (segmental) bronchus and a branch of the pulmonary artery at its center, whereas pulmonary vein tributaries run in the connective tissue septa between segments. This allows segments to be resected as units.

Q: During a hilar dissection, a surgeon identifies a nerve that passes posterior to the root of the lung. Which nerve is it?
A) Vagus nerve
B) Phrenic nerve
C) Recurrent laryngeal nerve
D) Sympathetic trunk
E) Greater splanchnic nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The vagus nerve passes posterior to the root of the lung and contributes to the posterior pulmonary plexus, whereas the phrenic nerve runs anterior to the root. The sympathetic trunk lies along the vertebral bodies and the splanchnic nerves lie medially on the vertebrae.

Q: During coronary artery bypass surgery, the surgeon needs to pass a clamp behind the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk. Which pericardial space is used?
A) Oblique pericardial sinus
B) Transverse pericardial sinus
C) Costomediastinal recess
D) Pericardiophrenic space
E) Pulmonary ligament space
Answer: B
Explanation: The transverse pericardial sinus lies posterior to the ascending aorta and pulmonary trunk and anterior to the superior vena cava and left atrium, allowing the great vessels to be clamped or ligated together. The oblique sinus lies behind the left atrium.

Q: A 55-year-old woman with mitral stenosis has dysphagia and a barium swallow that shows indentation of the esophagus at the level of the heart. Which cardiac chamber is responsible?
A) Right atrium
B) Left atrium
C) Right ventricle
D) Left ventricle
E) Left auricle only
Answer: B
Explanation: The left atrium forms most of the base (posterior surface) of the heart and lies directly anterior to the esophagus, so its enlargement can compress it. The right ventricle forms most of the anterior surface, and the right atrium forms the right border.

Q: A 62-year-old man has an inferior wall myocardial infarction with new atrioventricular block. Which artery is most likely occluded?
A) Right coronary artery
B) Anterior interventricular artery
C) Left circumflex artery
D) Left marginal artery
E) Left coronary artery proper
Answer: A
Explanation: The right coronary artery supplies the SA node in most people, the AV node in about 80%, and gives the posterior interventricular artery supplying the inferior wall and posterior septum. Its occlusion can produce inferior infarction and heart block.

Q: A 58-year-old man has a large anterior myocardial infarction. Which artery supplies the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum?
A) Anterior interventricular (left anterior descending) artery
B) Posterior interventricular artery
C) Left circumflex artery
D) Right marginal artery
E) Sinoatrial nodal artery
Answer: A
Explanation: The anterior interventricular (LAD) artery supplies the anterior two-thirds of the interventricular septum and the anterior walls of both ventricles, whereas the posterior interventricular artery supplies the posterior one-third. The circumflex artery supplies the left atrium and posterior left ventricle.

Q: Which of the following cardiac veins drains directly into the right atrium rather than through the coronary sinus?
A) Anterior cardiac vein
B) Great cardiac vein
C) Middle cardiac vein
D) Small cardiac vein
E) Posterior vein of the left ventricle
Answer: A
Explanation: The anterior cardiac veins drain the anterior right ventricle and open directly into the right atrium, as do the smallest cardiac veins. The great, middle, and small cardiac veins and the posterior vein of the left ventricle drain into the coronary sinus.

Q: A 70-year-old woman with sick sinus syndrome requires a pacemaker. Where is the natural pacemaker of the heart located?
A) Upper right atrium near the opening of the superior vena cava
B) Interatrial septum near the opening of the coronary sinus
C) Membranous interventricular septum
D) Left atrium near the pulmonary veins
E) Right ventricle in the septomarginal trabecula
Answer: A
Explanation: The SA node lies in the upper part of the sulcus terminalis, near the junction of the superior vena cava and right atrium. The AV node lies in the interatrial septum near the opening of the coronary sinus, and the bundle branches run in the interventricular septum.

Q: A 66-year-old man has a systolic ejection murmur due to aortic stenosis. Where is the murmur best heard on the chest wall?
A) Right second intercostal space near the sternum
B) Left second intercostal space near the sternum
C) Left fifth intercostal space at the lower sternal border
D) Left fifth intercostal space at the midclavicular line
E) Left third intercostal space at the sternal border
Answer: A
Explanation: The aortic valve is best auscultated at the right second intercostal space, the pulmonary valve at the left second intercostal space, the tricuspid valve at the left lower sternal border, and the mitral valve at the cardiac apex (left fifth intercostal space).

Q: A 40-year-old man with a deep vein thrombosis has a stroke from a paradoxical embolus. Which structure is the most likely route of embolus passage?
A) Probe-patent fossa ovalis in the interatrial septum
B) Membranous part of the interventricular septum
C) Ductus arteriosus
D) Crista terminalis
E) Orifice of the coronary sinus
Answer: A
Explanation: The fossa ovalis is the remnant of the foramen ovale in the interatrial septum; if incompletely closed, venous emboli can pass from the right to the left atrium and reach the systemic circulation, causing paradoxical embolism and stroke.

Q: Which structure carries the right bundle branch of the conducting system across the right ventricular cavity to the anterior papillary muscle?
A) Septomarginal trabecula (moderator band)
B) Crista supraventricularis
C) Chordae tendineae
D) Pectinate muscle
E) Posterior papillary muscle
Answer: A
Explanation: The septomarginal trabecula extends from the interventricular septum to the base of the anterior papillary muscle and carries the right bundle branch, helping coordinate contraction. Chordae tendineae anchor valve cusps to papillary muscles, and pectinate muscles are in the right atrium.

Q: A 64-year-old man has hoarseness. Chest radiography shows an aneurysm of the aortic arch. Around which structure does the left recurrent laryngeal nerve normally loop?
A) Left subclavian artery
B) Brachiocephalic trunk
C) Left pulmonary vein
D) Azygos arch
E) Aortic arch (at the ligamentum arteriosum)
Answer: E
Explanation: The left recurrent laryngeal nerve branches from the vagus at the aortic arch and passes beneath the arch lateral to the ligamentum arteriosum before ascending in the tracheoesophageal groove. An aneurysm can stretch it and cause hoarseness. The right recurrent nerve loops around the right subclavian artery.

Q: A 12-year-old boy has hypertension in the arms, weak femoral pulses, and notching of the inferior borders of the ribs on radiography. Which vessels enlarge to provide collateral flow through the thoracic wall to the descending aorta, in communication with the internal thoracic artery?
A) Bronchial arteries
B) Esophageal arteries
C) Posterior intercostal arteries
D) Lateral thoracic arteries
E) Pericardiacophrenic arteries
Answer: C
Explanation: In postductal coarctation of the aorta, collateral circulation develops from the subclavian artery through the internal thoracic arteries, anterior intercostal arteries, and posterior intercostal arteries into the descending aorta. The enlarged, tortuous intercostal arteries erode the costal grooves, producing rib notching.

Q: A tumor obstructs the inferior vena cava below the liver. Which vein serves as an important collateral pathway to the superior vena cava?
A) Azygos vein
B) Pulmonary vein
C) Portal vein
D) Coronary sinus
E) Hepatic vein
Answer: A
Explanation: The azygos system connects the inferior and superior vena cava through the lumbar veins and ascending lumbar veins, and the azygos vein arches over the root of the right lung to drain into the superior vena cava. This pathway enlarges when the IVC is obstructed.

Q: A 52-year-old man undergoes truncal vagotomy for refractory peptic ulcer disease. Which structures pass through the esophageal hiatus of the diaphragm together with the esophagus?
A) Thoracic duct
B) Azygos vein
C) Right phrenic nerve
D) Greater splanchnic nerves
E) Anterior and posterior vagal trunks
Answer: E
Explanation: The esophageal hiatus at about T10 transmits the esophagus, the anterior and posterior vagal trunks, and branches of the left gastric vessels. The aortic hiatus (T12) transmits the aorta, thoracic duct, and azygos vein, and the caval opening (T8) transmits the IVC and right phrenic nerve.

Q: A 3-year-old child swallows a coin that lodges at the level of the T4 vertebra on a chest radiograph. The esophagus is constricted at this level by which structures?
A) Diaphragm
B) Cricopharyngeus
C) Left atrium
D) Thoracic duct
E) Aortic arch and left main bronchus
Answer: E
Explanation: The esophagus has constrictions at its beginning (cricopharyngeus), where it is crossed by the aortic arch and left main bronchus, and where it passes through the diaphragm. The left atrium lies anterior to the esophagus at a lower level and may displace but does not constrict it normally.

Q: A patient with a cervical spinal cord injury at the C6 level continues to breathe spontaneously, whereas an injury at C2 would be fatal. From which spinal cord segments does the phrenic nerve arise?
A) C1, C2, and C3
B) C5, C6, and C7
C) T1, T2, and T3
D) C3, C4, and C5
E) C6, C7, and C8
Answer: D
Explanation: The phrenic nerve arises from the ventral rami of C3–C5 ("C3, 4, 5 keeps the diaphragm alive") and supplies the diaphragm. Injury above C3 interrupts the supply to the diaphragm bilaterally and causes respiratory failure.

Q: A radiograph of a 6-month-old infant shows a triangular "sail" shaped shadow adjacent to the heart. Where is the thymus normally located?
A) Superior and anterior mediastinum
B) Posterior mediastinum
C) Middle mediastinum
D) Inferior mediastinum behind the heart
E) Within the pericardial cavity
Answer: A
Explanation: The thymus lies in the superior and anterior mediastinum, behind the manubrium and anterior to the great vessels. It is relatively large in infants and children and gradually involutes after puberty.

Q: A 46-year-old man has a tumor invading the greater splanchnic nerve. Which of the following best describes this nerve?
A) It arises from L1–L2 and synapses in the inferior mesenteric ganglion
B) It carries postganglionic fibers from the sympathetic chain to the stomach
C) It carries preganglionic parasympathetic fibers to the foregut
D) It arises from S2–S4 and supplies the pelvic viscera
E) It arises from T5–T9 sympathetic ganglia, carries preganglionic fibers, and synapses in the celiac ganglion
Answer: E
Explanation: The greater splanchnic nerve (T5–T9) pierces the diaphragm and carries preganglionic sympathetic fibers and visceral afferents to the celiac ganglion, supplying foregut derivatives. The lesser splanchnic (T10–T11) goes to the aorticorenal ganglion, and the least splanchnic (T12) to the renal plexus.

Q: A 28-year-old woman has thoracic outlet syndrome from a cervical rib. Which structure normally passes between the anterior and middle scalene muscles together with the brachial plexus?
A) Subclavian vein
B) Phrenic nerve
C) Subclavian artery
D) Internal thoracic artery
E) Thoracic duct
Answer: C
Explanation: The subclavian artery and roots/trunks of the brachial plexus pass through the gap between the scalenus anterior and medius, whereas the subclavian vein passes anterior to the scalenus anterior. The phrenic nerve runs on the anterior surface of the scalenus anterior.

Q: A chest radiograph shows a tracheal bifurcation at the level of the sternal angle. Which other structure is located at the plane of the sternal angle?
A) Origin of the celiac trunk
B) Beginning and end of the aortic arch
C) Apex of the heart
D) Esophageal hiatus
E) Fifth lumbar vertebra
Answer: B
Explanation: The plane through the sternal angle and the T4–T5 intervertebral disc passes through the second costal cartilage, the beginning and end of the aortic arch, the tracheal bifurcation, the azygos arch entering the SVC, and the ligamentum arteriosum.

Q: A 30-year-old man is stabbed above the medial third of his clavicle and develops shortness of breath and a collapsed lung. Which structure was most likely penetrated?
A) Costodiaphragmatic recess
B) Costomediastinal recess
C) Pulmonary ligament
D) Oblique fissure
E) Cervical pleura and apex of the lung
Answer: E
Explanation: The cervical pleura and the apex of the lung extend above the first rib and the clavicle into the root of the neck, so a stab wound there can cause pneumothorax. The costodiaphragmatic and costomediastinal recesses lie at lower levels.

Q: A patient with asthma is given an anticholinergic bronchodilator. Parasympathetic stimulation of the lungs through which nerve normally causes bronchoconstriction and increased glandular secretion?
A) Phrenic nerve
B) Greater splanchnic nerve
C) Intercostal nerve
D) Long thoracic nerve
E) Vagus nerve
Answer: E
Explanation: Preganglionic parasympathetic fibers in the vagus nerve synapse in the pulmonary plexus and produce bronchoconstriction, vasodilation, and secretion; sympathetic fibers cause bronchodilation. The phrenic nerve is motor to the diaphragm.

Q: A surgeon harvests a vessel for coronary bypass that descends behind the costal cartilages about a finger breadth lateral to the sternum. Which artery is this, and from which vessel does it arise?
A) Internal thoracic artery from the subclavian artery
B) Superior epigastric artery from the external iliac artery
C) Lateral thoracic artery from the axillary artery
D) Posterior intercostal artery from the thoracic aorta
E) Musculophrenic artery from the brachiocephalic trunk
Answer: A
Explanation: The internal thoracic artery arises from the first part of the subclavian artery and descends behind the costal cartilages, giving anterior intercostal branches and ending by dividing into the superior epigastric and musculophrenic arteries. It is commonly used as a bypass graft to the LAD.

Q: A 50-year-old man has an abdominal incision below the arcuate line. In this region, the posterior surface of the rectus abdominis muscle is in direct contact with which layer?
A) Internal oblique aponeurosis
B) Transversus abdominis muscle
C) Transversalis fascia
D) External oblique muscle
E) Camper fascia
Answer: C
Explanation: Below the arcuate line, the aponeuroses of all three flat muscles pass anterior to the rectus abdominis, so the posterior rectus sheath is absent and the muscle lies directly on the transversalis fascia. Above the arcuate line, the posterior sheath is formed by the internal oblique and transversus abdominis aponeuroses.

Q: A 5-year-old boy has an indirect inguinal hernia. Which statement is correct?
A) The hernial sac passes medial to the inferior epigastric vessels through the inguinal triangle
B) The hernial sac is an acquired protrusion through a weakened posterior wall
C) The sac is outside the spermatic cord and its coverings
D) The sac passes through the femoral canal
E) The hernial sac passes lateral to the inferior epigastric vessels through the deep inguinal ring
Answer: E
Explanation: Indirect hernias enter the deep inguinal ring lateral to the inferior epigastric vessels, travel in the inguinal canal within the spermatic cord, and are usually congenital owing to a patent processus vaginalis. Direct hernias pass through the inguinal (Hesselbach) triangle medial to the vessels.

Q: During inguinal hernia repair, the surgeon identifies the cremasteric fascia of the spermatic cord. From which abdominal wall layer is this fascia derived?
A) External oblique aponeurosis
B) Transversalis fascia
C) Transversus abdominis muscle
D) Peritoneum
E) Internal oblique muscle
Answer: E
Explanation: The spermatic cord has three fascial coverings: external spermatic fascia from the external oblique aponeurosis, cremasteric muscle and fascia from the internal oblique, and internal spermatic fascia from the transversalis fascia.

Q: After repair of an inguinal hernia, a 40-year-old man has numbness over the scrotum and the upper medial thigh. Which nerve was most likely injured?
A) Genitofemoral nerve (genital branch)
B) Iliohypogastric nerve
C) Ilioinguinal nerve
D) Subcostal nerve
E) Obturator nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The ilioinguinal nerve (L1) passes through the inguinal canal on the spermatic cord and exits through the superficial inguinal ring to supply the skin of the anterior scrotum or labium majus and the upper medial thigh. The genital branch of the genitofemoral nerve supplies the cremaster.

Q: A 28-year-old man has a left-sided varicocele that does not decompress when he lies down. Which anatomic feature best explains the left-sided predominance?
A) The left testicular vein drains directly into the inferior vena cava
B) The left testicular artery is compressed by the sigmoid colon
C) The left testicular vein drains into the left renal vein
D) The left testicular vein drains into the portal vein
E) The left ductus deferens crosses the testicular vein
Answer: C
Explanation: The right testicular vein drains directly into the inferior vena cava, whereas the left drains into the left renal vein at a right angle, where pressure is higher. Obstruction of the left renal vein by a renal tumor may cause a persistent varicocele.

Q: A 25-year-old man has testicular carcinoma. To which lymph nodes would it first metastasize?
A) Superficial inguinal nodes
B) Deep inguinal nodes
C) External iliac nodes
D) Internal iliac nodes
E) Lumbar (para-aortic) nodes
Answer: E
Explanation: The testis develops near the kidney and retains its lymphatic drainage to the lumbar (para-aortic) nodes, whereas the scrotal skin drains to the superficial inguinal nodes. Testicular tumors therefore spread to the abdominal lymph nodes, not the inguinal nodes.

Q: During liver surgery, a surgeon compresses the free edge of the lesser omentum to control bleeding. Which structures are compressed?
A) Cystic artery, cystic duct, and hepatic vein
B) Splenic artery, splenic vein, and pancreatic tail
C) Left gastric artery, left gastric vein, and vagal trunk
D) Gastroduodenal artery, right gastric artery, and inferior vena cava
E) Hepatic artery proper, portal vein, and common bile duct
Answer: E
Explanation: The hepatoduodenal ligament, the free edge of the lesser omentum, contains the portal triad: the hepatic artery proper, portal vein, and common bile duct. Compression (the Pringle maneuver) at the epiploic foramen of Winslow controls hepatic bleeding. The inferior vena cava lies behind the foramen.

Q: A 55-year-old man with a posterior duodenal ulcer has sudden hematemesis. Which artery is most likely eroded?
A) Gastroduodenal artery
B) Left gastric artery
C) Splenic artery
D) Superior mesenteric artery
E) Right gastroepiploic artery
Answer: A
Explanation: The gastroduodenal artery runs behind the first part of the duodenum, so a posterior ulcer can erode it and cause severe hemorrhage. Anterior duodenal ulcers more often perforate into the peritoneal cavity.

Q: Which arteries supply the greater curvature of the stomach?
A) Right and left gastric arteries
B) Short gastric arteries only
C) Right and left gastro-omental (gastroepiploic) arteries
D) Superior and inferior pancreaticoduodenal arteries
E) Left gastric and splenic arteries
Answer: C
Explanation: The right gastro-omental artery arises from the gastroduodenal artery and the left from the splenic artery; both run along the greater curvature. The right and left gastric arteries supply the lesser curvature, and the short gastric arteries supply the fundus.

Q: A 65-year-old man has severe stenosis at the origin of the celiac trunk but no signs of foregut ischemia. Which anastomosis most likely provides collateral blood flow?
A) Between the right and left colic arteries
B) Between the middle colic and left colic arteries
C) Between the left gastric and splenic arteries
D) Between the internal iliac and inferior epigastric arteries
E) Between the superior and inferior pancreaticoduodenal arteries
Answer: E
Explanation: The superior pancreaticoduodenal arteries (from the celiac trunk via the gastroduodenal artery) anastomose with the inferior pancreaticoduodenal arteries (from the superior mesenteric artery), linking the foregut and midgut circulations.

Q: A 70-year-old man with atrial fibrillation has an embolus in the superior mesenteric artery. Which part of the gut would most likely be spared from infarction?
A) Jejunum
B) Ileum
C) Ascending colon
D) Descending colon
E) Proximal transverse colon
Answer: D
Explanation: The superior mesenteric artery supplies the duodenum distal to the major papilla through the proximal two-thirds of the transverse colon. The descending colon is supplied by the inferior mesenteric artery (left colic and sigmoid branches) and is spared.

Q: A 75-year-old woman with hypotension develops ischemic colitis at the splenic flexure. Which anatomic feature explains the vulnerability of this region?
A) It is supplied by a single end artery from the celiac trunk
B) It is the only retroperitoneal part of the colon
C) It lacks any venous drainage to the portal system
D) It lies at the watershed between the superior and inferior mesenteric artery territories
E) It is supplied only by the internal iliac artery
Answer: D
Explanation: The splenic flexure is a watershed zone where the middle colic artery (SMA) anastomoses with the left colic artery (IMA) through the marginal artery. Reduced flow may cause ischemia when this anastomosis is inadequate.

Q: A 19-year-old man has early appendicitis with vague pain around the umbilicus that later localizes to the right lower quadrant. At which spinal cord level do the visceral afferent fibers from the appendix enter?
A) T6
B) T12
C) L2
D) T10
E) S2
Answer: D
Explanation: Visceral pain from the midgut derivative appendix travels with sympathetic fibers and enters the spinal cord at T10, which is referred to the periumbilical dermatome. Later, inflammation of the parietal peritoneum causes localized somatic pain in the right lower quadrant.

Q: A surgeon identifies the ligamentum teres hepatis in the free edge of the falciform ligament. This ligament is a remnant of which fetal structure?
A) Ductus venosus
B) Umbilical artery
C) Urachus
D) Left umbilical vein
E) Right umbilical vein
Answer: D
Explanation: The ligamentum teres hepatis is the obliterated left umbilical vein, which carried oxygenated blood from the placenta to the fetus. The ductus venosus becomes the ligamentum venosum, the umbilical arteries become the medial umbilical ligaments, and the urachus becomes the median umbilical ligament.

Q: A 62-year-old man has painless jaundice and a palpable gallbladder. Imaging shows a mass in the head of the pancreas. Which structure is most likely compressed?
A) Splenic vein
B) Left renal vein
C) Celiac trunk
D) Common bile duct
E) Superior mesenteric artery
Answer: D
Explanation: The common bile duct runs behind or within the head of the pancreas before entering the duodenum, so a tumor in the head can obstruct it and cause painless obstructive jaundice. The splenic vein runs behind the body of the pancreas.

Q: A 55-year-old alcoholic man with cirrhosis has hematemesis from esophageal varices. Which anastomosis is responsible?
A) Superior rectal vein with middle rectal veins
B) Paraumbilical veins with superficial epigastric veins
C) Colic veins with retroperitoneal veins
D) Splenic vein with left renal vein
E) Left gastric vein with the esophageal veins draining to the azygos system
Answer: E
Explanation: In portal hypertension, blood is shunted from the portal system (left gastric vein) to the systemic system (esophageal veins draining into the azygos vein), dilating submucosal esophageal veins into varices that may rupture.

Q: A 50-year-old man with portal hypertension has dilated veins radiating from the umbilicus (caput medusae). Which veins connect the portal and caval systems in this anastomosis?
A) Left gastric and esophageal veins
B) Superior and inferior rectal veins
C) Right colic and lumbar veins
D) Paraumbilical veins and superficial epigastric veins
E) Splenic and left renal veins
Answer: D
Explanation: Paraumbilical veins along the ligamentum teres hepatis connect the left portal vein with the superficial epigastric veins of the abdominal wall, which drain to the femoral and axillary veins. Increased portal pressure dilates these veins.

Q: During laparoscopic cholecystectomy, a surgeon dissects the cystohepatic (Calot) triangle and encounters the cystic artery. From which artery does it usually arise?
A) Right hepatic artery
B) Left hepatic artery
C) Gastroduodenal artery
D) Right gastric artery
E) Splenic artery
Answer: A
Explanation: The cystic artery usually arises from the right hepatic artery in the triangle bounded by the cystic duct, common hepatic duct, and the inferior surface of the liver. Identification prevents inadvertent ligation of the hepatic artery or bile duct.

Q: A 45-year-old woman has gallstone pancreatitis from a stone impacted at the hepatopancreatic ampulla. In which part of the duodenum does the ampulla open?
A) Second (descending) part
B) First (superior) part
C) Third (horizontal) part
D) Fourth (ascending) part
E) Duodenojejunal flexure
Answer: A
Explanation: The common bile duct and main pancreatic duct join in the hepatopancreatic ampulla and open on the major duodenal papilla in the descending (second) part of the duodenum. Obstruction can cause jaundice and pancreatitis.

Q: During splenectomy for traumatic rupture, which structure within the splenorenal ligament is at risk of injury?
A) Head of the pancreas
B) Common bile duct
C) Left gastric artery
D) Right gastroepiploic artery
E) Tail of the pancreas
Answer: E
Explanation: The splenorenal ligament contains the splenic vessels and the tail of the pancreas, which may be damaged during splenectomy, causing a pancreatic fistula. The gastrosplenic ligament contains the short gastric and left gastro-omental vessels.

Q: A 30-year-old woman has vomiting and weight loss after a rapid weight reduction. Imaging shows compression of the duodenum between the superior mesenteric artery and the aorta. Which part of the duodenum is compressed?
A) First part
B) Second part
C) Third (horizontal) part
D) Duodenal cap
E) Duodenojejunal flexure only
Answer: C
Explanation: The third part of the duodenum crosses the midline between the aorta posteriorly and the superior mesenteric artery and vein anteriorly; loss of retroperitoneal fat may narrow this space and compress the duodenum.

Q: A 40-year-old man has renal colic from a stone in the ureter. Which site is the narrowest part of the ureter and a common place for stones to lodge?
A) Mid-abdomen at the psoas
B) Level of the renal pelvis
C) Crossing of the testicular vein
D) Ureterovesical junction
E) Crossing of the gonadal artery
Answer: D
Explanation: The ureter has three constrictions: at the ureteropelvic junction, where it crosses the pelvic brim over the iliac vessels, and at the ureterovesical junction as it passes through the bladder wall, which is the narrowest. Pain radiates from the flank to the groin.

Q: A surgeon performing a right adrenalectomy must carefully control the right suprarenal vein. Where does this vein drain?
A) Right renal vein
B) Directly into the inferior vena cava
C) Right inferior phrenic vein
D) Portal vein
E) Azygos vein
Answer: B
Explanation: The right suprarenal vein is short and enters the inferior vena cava directly, making it prone to tearing. The left suprarenal vein drains into the left renal vein.

Q: A 35-year-old woman has a pheochromocytoma. Which statement about the innervation of the adrenal medulla is correct?
A) Chromaffin cells are innervated by postganglionic sympathetic fibers
B) Chromaffin cells are innervated by parasympathetic fibers of the vagus
C) Chromaffin cells are innervated directly by preganglionic sympathetic fibers
D) Chromaffin cells are innervated by somatic motor nerves
E) Chromaffin cells receive no innervation
Answer: C
Explanation: The suprarenal medulla is a modified sympathetic ganglion whose chromaffin cells are innervated directly by preganglionic sympathetic fibers from the greater splanchnic nerve and release epinephrine and norepinephrine into the blood.

Q: A vascular surgeon plans an aortic aneurysm repair and reviews the origins of the major branches. At which vertebral level does the celiac trunk arise?
A) L1
B) L2
C) T12
D) L3
E) L4
Answer: C
Explanation: The celiac trunk arises at T12, the superior mesenteric artery at L1, the renal arteries at L1–L2, the inferior mesenteric artery at L3, and the aorta bifurcates at L4.

Q: An obese 45-year-old man has burning pain and numbness over the anterolateral thigh that worsens when he wears a tight belt. Which nerve is compressed as it passes behind the inguinal ligament?
A) Lateral femoral cutaneous nerve
B) Femoral nerve
C) Genitofemoral nerve
D) Ilioinguinal nerve
E) Obturator nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The lateral femoral cutaneous nerve (L2–L3) emerges from the lateral border of the psoas, crosses the iliacus, and passes behind the inguinal ligament to supply the skin of the anterolateral thigh. Its compression causes pain and paresthesia without motor loss.

Q: A neurologist tests the cremasteric reflex in a 20-year-old man by stroking the skin of the medial thigh. Which nerve carries the efferent limb?
A) Genital branch of the genitofemoral nerve
B) Femoral branch of the genitofemoral nerve
C) Ilioinguinal nerve
D) Iliohypogastric nerve
E) Pudendal nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The afferent limb of the cremasteric reflex is the femoral branch of the genitofemoral nerve, and the efferent limb is its genital branch, which causes the cremaster to contract and elevate the testis.

Q: A 20-year-old man has painless rectal bleeding. A technetium scan shows ectopic gastric mucosa in an ileal outpouching. Which statement about this structure is correct?
A) It is a remnant of the urachus attached to the umbilicus
B) It arises from the antimesenteric side of the jejunum
C) It is a remnant of the ductus venosus
D) It is a remnant of the yolk stalk located about 2 ft from the ileocecal junction
E) It is located on the mesenteric border of the cecum
Answer: D
Explanation: Meckel diverticulum is a persistent part of the vitelline duct (yolk stalk), arising from the antimesenteric border of the ileum about 2 ft proximal to the ileocecal junction, about 2 in. long, in about 2% of the population. Ectopic gastric mucosa may cause ulceration and bleeding.

Q: A 45-year-old man has chronic constipation and fecal incontinence after pelvic surgery. Which muscle forms a sling around the anorectal junction and is important for maintaining fecal continence?
A) Iliococcygeus
B) Puborectalis
C) Coccygeus
D) Obturator internus
E) Piriformis
Answer: B
Explanation: The puborectalis part of the levator ani forms a sling around the anorectal junction, maintaining the anorectal angle and helping fecal continence. The iliococcygeus and pubococcygeus support the pelvic floor, and the coccygeus lies on the sacrospinous ligament.

Q: A 50-year-old woman has painless rectal bleeding from internal hemorrhoids above the pectinate line. Why are these hemorrhoids painless?
A) This region is supplied by the inferior rectal nerves
B) This region is supplied by the pudendal nerve
C) The veins have no nerve supply
D) The region is drained by the inferior rectal veins
E) This region is supplied by visceral afferent fibers insensitive to cutting and burning
Answer: E
Explanation: The anal canal above the pectinate line is derived from the hindgut, is innervated by visceral (autonomic) fibers, and is sensitive only to stretch. Below the pectinate line, the somatic inferior rectal nerve makes external hemorrhoids painful. Internal hemorrhoids drain by the superior rectal vein to the portal system.

Q: A 60-year-old man has squamous cell carcinoma of the anal canal below the pectinate line. To which lymph nodes would it first metastasize?
A) Internal iliac nodes
B) Inferior mesenteric nodes
C) Lumbar nodes
D) Superficial inguinal nodes
E) Deep inguinal nodes only
Answer: D
Explanation: Lymph from the anal canal below the pectinate line drains to the superficial inguinal nodes, whereas lymph from above the line drains to the internal iliac and inferior mesenteric nodes.

Q: A 35-year-old man has a recurrent perianal abscess in the ischioanal fossa that has spread to the opposite side. Through which route does the infection most likely cross the midline?
A) Perineal membrane
B) Deep postanal space behind the anal canal
C) Urogenital diaphragm
D) Obturator canal
E) Superficial perineal pouch
Answer: B
Explanation: The two ischioanal fossae communicate behind the anal canal through the deep postanal space, so an abscess can spread to the opposite side as a horseshoe abscess. The fossa contains fat, the inferior rectal vessels and nerves, and the pudendal canal.

Q: A 22-year-old man falls astride a bar and ruptures the bulbous (spongy) urethra below the perineal membrane. Urine extravasates into the scrotum and the anterior abdominal wall but not into the thigh. What explains the limited spread?
A) Scarpa fascia attaches to the fascia lata of the thigh and Colles fascia is separate from it
B) Colles fascia is continuous with Scarpa fascia and attaches to the ischiopubic rami and perineal membrane
C) The perineal membrane is incomplete anteriorly
D) The deep perineal pouch communicates with the thigh
E) The ischioanal fossa communicates with the thigh
Answer: B
Explanation: Urine leaking from the spongy urethra enters the superficial perineal pouch, bounded by the perineal membrane and Colles fascia (the membranous layer of the superficial perineal fascia), and spreads to the scrotum, penis, and lower anterior abdominal wall deep to Scarpa fascia. Fascial attachments to the ischiopubic rami and fascia lata prevent spread to the thigh.

Q: A 30-year-old man with a pelvic fracture has blood at the urethral meatus and a high-riding prostate. Which part of the urethra is most likely ruptured?
A) Prostatic urethra
B) Bulbous urethra
C) Penile urethra
D) Membranous urethra
E) Navicular fossa
Answer: D
Explanation: The membranous urethra, the narrowest and least distensible part, passes through the deep perineal pouch and is easily torn in pelvic fractures. Extravasated urine enters the deep perineal pouch and may spread into the retropubic and pelvic spaces. The bulbous urethra is typically torn in straddle injuries.

Q: A 68-year-old man has a hard nodule palpated on digital rectal examination. Which part of the prostate lies against the anterior rectal wall and is the most common site of carcinoma?
A) Posterior (peripheral) part
B) Median lobe
C) Anterior fibromuscular part
D) Periurethral transition area
E) Apex only
Answer: A
Explanation: The posterior part of the prostate lies directly against the rectal ampulla and is palpable on rectal examination; most prostatic carcinomas arise in this peripheral zone. Benign hyperplasia typically involves the periurethral and median regions and obstructs the urethra.

Q: A 70-year-old man with prostate cancer has back pain and radiographs show metastases in the lumbar vertebrae. Through which route did cancer cells most likely spread?
A) Prostatic veins to the portal vein
B) Hepatic veins to the vena cava
C) Lymphatics along the inferior epigastric vessels
D) Direct extension through the pelvic floor
E) Prostatic venous plexus to the internal vertebral venous plexus
Answer: E
Explanation: The prostatic and vesical venous plexuses communicate with the vertebral venous plexus, which is valveless, allowing metastasis to the vertebral column, spinal cord, and brain. Prostate cancer may also spread to the lungs through the internal iliac veins and inferior vena cava.

Q: A urologist describes the formation of the ejaculatory duct. Which structures unite to form it?
A) Ductus deferens and bulbourethral duct
B) Ductus deferens and duct of the seminal vesicle
C) Epididymis and prostatic duct
D) Seminal vesicle and urethral gland
E) Ductus deferens and efferent ductules
Answer: B
Explanation: The ejaculatory duct is formed by the union of the ductus deferens and the duct of the seminal vesicle; it passes through the prostate and opens into the prostatic urethra at the seminal colliculus.

Q: A 58-year-old multiparous woman has a sensation of heaviness and a cervix protruding at the vaginal introitus. Weakness of which structures is most important in uterine prolapse?
A) Round ligaments of the uterus
B) Ovarian ligaments
C) Cardinal ligaments and levator ani muscles
D) Broad ligaments only
E) Suspensory ligaments of the ovary
Answer: C
Explanation: The cardinal (transverse cervical) ligaments, together with the uterosacral ligaments and the levator ani, are the major supports of the uterus; their weakness after childbirth allows uterine prolapse. The round ligament maintains anteversion, and the broad ligament is a peritoneal fold.

Q: During a hysterectomy, a surgeon ligates the uterine artery close to the cervix. Which structure lies immediately beneath the artery and is at risk of injury?
A) Ductus deferens
B) Obturator nerve
C) Internal iliac vein
D) Inferior vesical artery
E) Ureter
Answer: E
Explanation: The ureter passes under the uterine artery ("water under the bridge") about 2 cm lateral to the supravaginal part of the cervix. Ligature of the artery may therefore injure the ureter. In males, the ductus deferens crosses over the ureter.

Q: During an oophorectomy, a surgeon clamps the suspensory ligament of the ovary. Which structures are contained within it?
A) Uterine artery and vein
B) Round ligament of the uterus
C) Ureter and uterine artery
D) Ovarian ligament and uterine tube
E) Ovarian vessels, nerves, and lymphatics
Answer: E
Explanation: The suspensory ligament of the ovary (infundibulopelvic ligament) is a fold of peritoneum that contains the ovarian artery, vein, nerves, and lymphatics. The ovarian ligament connects the ovary to the uterus, and the round ligament passes to the labium majus.

Q: A 62-year-old woman has carcinoma of the fundus of the uterus. Metastasis to which group of nodes could occur by way of lymphatics that follow the round ligament?
A) Sacral nodes
B) Superficial inguinal nodes
C) Internal iliac nodes only
D) Popliteal nodes
E) Deep inguinal nodes only
Answer: B
