Explanation: Most uterine lymph drains to the iliac and lumbar nodes, but a few vessels from the fundus follow the round ligament through the inguinal canal to the superficial inguinal nodes. The cervix drains mainly to the internal and external iliac and sacral nodes.

Q: A 55-year-old man has loss of ejaculation after retroperitoneal surgery that injured the sympathetic nerves, but erection is preserved. Which statement best explains his findings?
A) Erection is mediated by sympathetic nerves, and ejaculation by parasympathetic nerves
B) Both erection and ejaculation are mediated by the pudendal nerve
C) Erection is mediated by parasympathetic pelvic splanchnic nerves, and ejaculation by sympathetic nerves
D) Both are mediated by sympathetic nerves only
E) Erection is mediated by the ilioinguinal nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: Erection depends on parasympathetic fibers from the pelvic splanchnic nerves (S2–S4), producing vasodilation of the erectile tissue; emission and ejaculation depend on sympathetic fibers (lumbar splanchnic and hypogastric nerves), with somatic pudendal nerve fibers for the bulbospongiosus contraction.

Q: A 50-year-old woman with a spinal cord lesion at S2–S4 has urinary retention with a flaccid bladder. Which nerves normally cause contraction of the detrusor muscle?
A) Hypogastric nerves
B) Pelvic splanchnic nerves
C) Pudendal nerves
D) Lumbar splanchnic nerves
E) Genitofemoral nerves
Answer: B
Explanation: Parasympathetic pelvic splanchnic nerves (S2–S4) cause contraction of the detrusor and relaxation of the internal urethral sphincter for micturition. Sympathetic fibers inhibit the detrusor and contract the internal sphincter, and the pudendal nerve controls the external urethral sphincter.

Q: A 30-year-old woman has pelvic inflammatory disease with a pus collection when she stands or sits. Where does the fluid most likely collect?
A) Vesicouterine pouch
B) Paravesical fossa
C) Subphrenic space
D) Retropubic space
E) Rectouterine pouch
Answer: E
Explanation: The rectouterine pouch (of Douglas) is the lowest part of the peritoneal cavity in the female in the upright or sitting position; fluid and pus collect there and can be drained through the posterior fornix of the vagina (culdocentesis). The male equivalent is the rectovesical pouch.

Q: A surgeon ligates the internal iliac artery to control pelvic hemorrhage. Which of the following arteries arises from its posterior division?
A) Superior gluteal artery
B) Uterine artery
C) Middle rectal artery
D) Internal pudendal artery
E) Obturator artery
Answer: A
Explanation: The posterior division of the internal iliac artery gives the iliolumbar, lateral sacral, and superior gluteal arteries. The uterine, vaginal, vesical, middle rectal, internal pudendal, obturator, and inferior gluteal arteries arise from the anterior division.

Q: A 40-year-old man has a distended bladder that rises above the pubic symphysis. A urologist performs a suprapubic puncture. Why can the bladder be punctured here without entering the peritoneal cavity?
A) The bladder is intraperitoneal in all positions
B) The peritoneum covers the anterior surface of the bladder
C) The peritoneum is displaced upward from the anterior abdominal wall by the distended bladder
D) The bladder lies within the rectus sheath
E) The urachus protects the peritoneum
Answer: C
Explanation: A full bladder rises above the pubic symphysis, lifting the peritoneum off the lower anterior abdominal wall so that the needle enters the bladder extraperitoneally. The superior surface of the empty bladder is covered by peritoneum.

Q: A newborn has a normal examination except for a tuft of hair over the lumbosacral region. Radiography shows an unfused vertebral arch at L5. Which condition is this?
A) Meningocele
B) Meningomyelocele
C) Myeloschisis
D) Spina bifida occulta
E) Syringomyelia
Answer: D
Explanation: In spina bifida occulta, the vertebral arch fails to fuse but the meninges and spinal cord remain within the vertebral canal, often marked by a tuft of hair or dimple. Meningocele and meningomyelocele involve a cystic protrusion of meninges (with or without neural tissue).

Q: A 28-year-old man has a hyperflexion injury of the neck with a fracture of the dens. Which ligament normally holds the dens against the anterior arch of the atlas?
A) Alar ligament
B) Apical ligament
C) Anterior longitudinal ligament
D) Transverse ligament of the atlas
E) Ligamentum flavum
Answer: D
Explanation: The transverse ligament of the atlas holds the dens against the anterior arch of the atlas and, with its superior and inferior bands, forms the cruciate ligament. Its rupture permits displacement of the dens and spinal cord compression. The alar ligaments limit rotation.

Q: A physician palpates a prominent spinous process at the base of the neck. Which vertebra has the long, nonbifid spinous process known as the vertebra prominens?
A) C2
B) C7
C) C5
D) T1
E) T4
Answer: B
Explanation: The seventh cervical vertebra has a long, nonbifid spinous process that is easily palpated at the base of the neck. The spinous processes of C2–C6 are short and usually bifid, and T1 also has a long process but it is less prominent.

Q: A 50-year-old woman undergoes lumbar puncture at the L3–L4 interspace. Why is this level selected in an adult?
A) The spinal cord extends to L4 in adults
B) The epidural space is absent at this level
C) The spinal cord ends at about L1–L2, and the subarachnoid space extends to S2
D) The dura mater ends at L3
E) The filum terminale ends at L3
Answer: C
Explanation: In the adult, the spinal cord ends at the conus medullaris near the L1–L2 level, but the subarachnoid space extends to S2, so a needle at L3–L4 or L4–L5 enters the lumbar cistern containing the cauda equina and cerebrospinal fluid without damaging the cord. The supracristal line passes through L4.

Q: A woman in labor receives caudal anesthesia. Through which structure does the needle pass to enter the sacral canal?
A) Ligamentum flavum at L4
B) Sacrococcygeal ligament over the sacral hiatus
C) Posterior longitudinal ligament
D) Supraspinous ligament at L2
E) Posterior sacroiliac ligament
Answer: B
Explanation: The sacral hiatus, bounded by the sacral cornua, is covered by the sacrococcygeal ligament; a needle passed through it enters the epidural space of the sacral canal, which contains the sacral nerve roots and fat.

Q: A 40-year-old man with severe back pain has a posterolateral herniation of the L4–L5 intervertebral disc. Which nerve root is most likely compressed?
A) L4
B) S1
C) L3
D) S2
E) L5
Answer: E
Explanation: Because of the oblique descent of the lower lumbar roots, a posterolateral herniation of the L4–L5 disc usually compresses the L5 root, which passes down across the disc, not the L4 root that exits at that level. Herniation is posterolateral because the posterior longitudinal ligament is thinner laterally.

Q: A 35-year-old woman in a rear-end car collision has a hyperextension injury of her neck. Which ligament is most likely torn?
A) Anterior longitudinal ligament
B) Posterior longitudinal ligament
C) Ligamentum flavum
D) Supraspinous ligament
E) Interspinous ligament
Answer: A
Explanation: The anterior longitudinal ligament limits extension of the vertebral column and is injured in whiplash. The posterior longitudinal ligament, ligamentum flavum, and supraspinous and interspinous ligaments resist hyperflexion.

Q: A neurosurgeon operating in the suboccipital region identifies the suboccipital triangle. Which structure lies within it?
A) Occipital artery only
B) Internal carotid artery
C) Greater occipital nerve only
D) Spinal accessory nerve
E) Vertebral artery
Answer: E
Explanation: The suboccipital triangle is bounded by the rectus capitis posterior major and the obliquus capitis superior and inferior, and it contains the third part of the vertebral artery and the suboccipital nerve (dorsal ramus of C1). The greater occipital nerve (C2) lies just below the triangle.

Q: A 55-year-old man has paralysis and loss of pain and temperature sensation below the level of a lesion after occlusion of the anterior spinal artery, but vibration and position sense are intact. Which part of the cord does this artery supply?
A) Posterior one-third of the spinal cord
B) Anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord
C) Dorsal columns only
D) Posterior horns only
E) Lateral horns only
Answer: B
Explanation: The anterior spinal artery arises from the vertebral arteries and supplies the anterior two-thirds of the spinal cord, including the corticospinal and spinothalamic tracts; the two posterior spinal arteries supply the posterior one-third, including the dorsal columns that carry vibration and proprioception.

Q: A neuroanatomist identifies the denticulate ligaments of the spinal cord. These ligaments are specializations of which meningeal layer?
A) Dura mater
B) Arachnoid mater
C) Pia mater
D) Epidural fat
E) Filum terminale
Answer: C
Explanation: Denticulate ligaments are lateral extensions of the pia mater that attach to the dura mater between the dorsal and ventral roots and suspend the spinal cord within the subarachnoid space. The filum terminale is a pial extension anchoring the conus medullaris to the coccyx.

Q: A 45-year-old man has pain at the back of the head. The greater occipital nerve is a branch of which structure?
A) Ventral ramus of C2
B) Dorsal ramus of C1
C) Dorsal ramus of C2
D) Ventral ramus of C3
E) Spinal accessory nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The greater occipital nerve is the dorsal ramus of C2 and supplies the skin of the posterior scalp up to the vertex. The suboccipital nerve is the dorsal ramus of C1, and the lesser occipital nerve is a branch of the cervical plexus (ventral rami).

Q: A 38-year-old man has a lumbar spinal nerve compression. Which spinal nerve emerges through the intervertebral foramen between the L4 and L5 vertebrae?
A) L4
B) L5
C) L3
D) S1
E) C8
Answer: A
Explanation: Lumbar and sacral spinal nerves exit below the vertebra of the same number, so the L4 nerve passes through the intervertebral foramen between L4 and L5. Only the cervical nerves (C1–C7) exit above the corresponding vertebra, and C8 exits between C7 and T1.

Q: A 16-year-old gymnast has low back pain, and radiographs show forward slippage of L5 on S1 after a defect in the pars interarticularis. What is this condition called?
A) Spondylolysis only
B) Spondylolisthesis
C) Scoliosis
D) Kyphosis
E) Ankylosing spondylitis
Answer: B
Explanation: Spondylolisthesis is the forward displacement of a vertebra (usually L5 over S1) after bilateral fracture (spondylolysis) of the pars interarticularis. It may compress the cauda equina or lumbar nerve roots.

Q: A 50-year-old man has a lymph node biopsy in the posterior triangle of the neck. Afterward, he cannot shrug his shoulder and his shoulder droops. Which nerve was injured?
A) Long thoracic nerve
B) Spinal accessory nerve
C) Dorsal scapular nerve
D) Phrenic nerve
E) Suprascapular nerve
Answer: B
Explanation: The spinal accessory nerve (CN XI) runs superficially in the roof of the posterior triangle and supplies the sternocleidomastoid and trapezius. Its injury causes weakness of shoulder elevation and drooping. The long thoracic nerve supplies the serratus anterior.

Q: During a carotid endarterectomy, a nerve crossing the internal and external carotid arteries is injured. After surgery, the tongue deviates to the side of the lesion on protrusion. Which nerve was injured?
A) Hypoglossal nerve
B) Glossopharyngeal nerve
C) Vagus nerve
D) Lingual nerve
E) Spinal accessory nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The hypoglossal nerve loops across the internal and external carotid arteries in the carotid triangle. Paralysis of the genioglossus causes the protruded tongue to deviate toward the injured side. The lingual nerve carries general sensation from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue.

Q: Massage of the carotid sinus in a 70-year-old man slows his heart rate. Which cranial nerve carries the afferent fibers from this baroreceptor?
A) Vagus nerve
B) Trigeminal nerve
C) Glossopharyngeal nerve
D) Facial nerve
E) Hypoglossal nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The carotid sinus is supplied mainly by the carotid sinus nerve, a branch of the glossopharyngeal nerve (CN IX), which carries afferent impulses to the medulla; the vagus provides the efferent limb to slow the heart. The carotid body is also innervated by the glossopharyngeal and vagus nerves.

Q: An angiogram shows the carotid arteries in the neck of a 60-year-old man. Which of the following statements about the internal carotid artery in the neck is correct?
A) It gives off no branches in the neck
B) It gives off the superior thyroid artery
C) It gives off the lingual artery
D) It gives off the facial artery
E) It gives off the ascending pharyngeal artery
Answer: A
Explanation: The internal carotid artery has no branches in the neck and enters the skull through the carotid canal, whereas the external carotid artery gives off the superior thyroid, ascending pharyngeal, lingual, facial, occipital, posterior auricular, maxillary, and superficial temporal arteries.

Q: A professional singer notices voice fatigue and inability to reach high notes after a thyroidectomy in which the superior thyroid artery was ligated near the gland. Which nerve was injured?
A) Internal laryngeal nerve
B) Recurrent laryngeal nerve
C) Hypoglossal nerve
D) External laryngeal nerve
E) Glossopharyngeal nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The external laryngeal nerve runs with the superior thyroid artery and supplies the cricothyroid muscle, which tenses the vocal folds for high-pitched sounds. The internal laryngeal nerve is sensory above the vocal folds, and the recurrent laryngeal nerve supplies the other intrinsic muscles.

Q: A 45-year-old woman develops tingling around the mouth and carpopedal spasm after thyroidectomy. Which artery usually supplies the parathyroid glands?
A) Superior thyroid artery
B) Inferior thyroid artery
C) Thyroid ima artery
D) Ascending pharyngeal artery
E) Lingual artery
Answer: B
Explanation: The parathyroid glands, on the posterior surface of the thyroid, are supplied mainly by the inferior thyroid artery (from the thyrocervical trunk); their removal or devascularization causes hypocalcemia and tetany.

Q: A 6-year-old boy has a midline neck mass that moves upward when he protrudes his tongue. Which embryologic structure is the likely origin?
A) Second pharyngeal cleft
B) Third pharyngeal pouch
C) Persistent cervical sinus
D) Thyroglossal duct remnant
E) Lymph node
Answer: D
Explanation: The thyroid gland develops from the floor of the pharynx at the foramen cecum and descends in the neck through the thyroglossal duct, which normally disappears. A persistent remnant forms a midline cyst that moves with swallowing and protrusion of the tongue.

Q: A newborn has hypocalcemic tetany and recurrent infections due to absence of the thymus and parathyroid glands. Which embryonic structures failed to develop normally?
A) First and second pharyngeal pouches
B) Third and fourth pharyngeal pouches
C) First pharyngeal arch only
D) Second pharyngeal cleft
E) Fifth pharyngeal pouch only
Answer: B
Explanation: The third pouch forms the thymus and inferior parathyroid glands, and the fourth pouch forms the superior parathyroid glands and ultimobranchial body. Defective development produces absent parathyroids and thymus with hypocalcemia and immunodeficiency.

Q: A 9-year-old boy has a retropharyngeal abscess. Into which region can infection spread by way of this space?
A) Anterior mediastinum
B) Posterior mediastinum
C) Pleural cavity directly
D) Pericardial cavity
E) Subdural space
Answer: B
Explanation: The retropharyngeal space lies between the buccopharyngeal and prevertebral fasciae and continues inferiorly into the posterior mediastinum, so infection can descend into the chest.

Q: A surgeon identifies the branches of the thyrocervical trunk. Which artery arises from it?
A) Vertebral artery
B) Internal thoracic artery
C) Costocervical trunk
D) Suprascapular artery
E) Dorsal scapular artery in all cases
Answer: D
Explanation: The thyrocervical trunk arises from the first part of the subclavian artery and gives off the inferior thyroid, suprascapular, and transverse cervical arteries. The vertebral and internal thoracic arteries arise separately from the first part, and the costocervical trunk from the second part.

Q: During an interscalene brachial plexus block, the physician is concerned about transient diaphragmatic paralysis. Which nerve lies on the anterior surface of the scalenus anterior muscle?
A) Long thoracic nerve
B) Vagus nerve
C) Spinal accessory nerve
D) Phrenic nerve
E) Suprascapular nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The phrenic nerve descends on the anterior surface of the scalenus anterior beneath the prevertebral fascia and may be anesthetized by interscalene block. The brachial plexus roots and trunks pass between the anterior and middle scalenes.

Q: A 40-year-old man has a parotid tumor excised. Afterward, the corner of his mouth droops on one side and the lower lip does not move normally. Which branch of the facial nerve was injured?
A) Temporal branch
B) Marginal mandibular branch
C) Zygomatic branch
D) Buccal branch
E) Cervical branch
Answer: B
Explanation: The marginal mandibular branch supplies the muscles of the lower lip and chin (depressor anguli oris and others). The facial nerve branches within the parotid gland are temporal, zygomatic, buccal, marginal mandibular, and cervical.

Q: A 35-year-old woman wakes with inability to close her right eye, wrinkle her forehead, or smile on the right, and has reduced taste on the anterior two-thirds of the tongue. Which finding distinguishes this lesion from a cerebral stroke?
A) Paralysis of the forehead muscles
B) Loss of taste on the anterior tongue
C) Drooping of the mouth
D) Inability to smile
E) Loss of facial expression below the eye
Answer: A
Explanation: A lower motor neuron lesion of the facial nerve (Bell palsy) paralyzes the whole ipsilateral face including the forehead, because the nucleus is destroyed. In an upper motor neuron lesion (stroke), the forehead is spared since it has bilateral cortical innervation.

Q: A 12-year-old boy has painful swelling of the parotid gland (mumps). Which nerve carries the preganglionic secretomotor fibers from the glossopharyngeal nerve to the otic ganglion?
A) Greater petrosal nerve
B) Chorda tympani
C) Deep petrosal nerve
D) Lesser petrosal nerve
E) Auriculotemporal nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The glossopharyngeal nerve gives rise to the tympanic nerve, which becomes the lesser petrosal nerve and synapses in the otic ganglion; postganglionic fibers travel with the auriculotemporal nerve to the parotid gland. The greater petrosal nerve carries facial parasympathetic fibers to the pterygopalatine ganglion.

Q: A 70-year-old woman with osteoporosis has an exaggerated thoracic curvature. Which pair of vertebral column curvatures is primary (present at birth) and concave anteriorly?
A) Cervical and lumbar curvatures
B) Thoracic and sacral curvatures
C) Cervical and thoracic curvatures
D) Lumbar and sacral curvatures
E) Cervical and sacral curvatures
Answer: B
Explanation: The thoracic and sacral curvatures are primary curvatures (kyphotic, concave anteriorly) present in the fetus. The cervical and lumbar curvatures are secondary (lordotic) curvatures that develop after birth as the infant lifts the head and begins to stand. Exaggerated thoracic curvature is kyphosis.

Q: A 55-year-old man has an infected pimple on the scalp and later develops signs of meningitis. Through which scalp layer does infection spread most readily, entering the cranial cavity through emissary veins?
A) Skin
B) Dense connective tissue
C) Epicranial aponeurosis
D) Loose areolar connective tissue
E) Pericranium
Answer: D
Explanation: The layers of the scalp are skin, connective tissue, aponeurosis (galea), loose areolar tissue, and pericranium (SCALP). The loose areolar layer is the "danger area" because infection spreads easily through it and through valveless emissary veins into the dural venous sinuses.

Q: A 20-year-old man squeezes a pustule on his upper lip and develops fever, proptosis, and ophthalmoplegia due to cavernous sinus thrombosis. Which route did the infection most likely take?
A) Facial vein to retromandibular vein to external jugular vein
B) Maxillary vein to pterygoid venous plexus to internal jugular vein
C) Facial vein to internal jugular vein
D) Superficial temporal vein to transverse sinus
E) Facial vein to angular vein to superior ophthalmic vein
Answer: E
Explanation: The facial vein communicates with the cavernous sinus through the angular vein and the superior ophthalmic vein, and these veins have no valves, so infections of the "danger triangle" of the face may spread intracranially.

Q: A neurologist tests sensation over the lower jaw and weakness of mastication. Through which foramen does the mandibular division of the trigeminal nerve leave the skull?
A) Foramen rotundum
B) Foramen spinosum
C) Superior orbital fissure
D) Foramen lacerum
E) Foramen ovale
Answer: E
Explanation: The mandibular nerve (V3) passes through the foramen ovale, the maxillary nerve (V2) through the foramen rotundum, and the ophthalmic nerve (V1) through the superior orbital fissure. The foramen spinosum transmits the middle meningeal artery.

Q: A 22-year-old man has a lucid interval after a blow to the temple, then deteriorates. CT shows a lens-shaped epidural hematoma beneath a fracture of the pterion. Which vessel was torn?
A) Superficial temporal artery
B) Bridging cerebral vein
C) Middle meningeal artery
D) Superior sagittal sinus
E) Middle cerebral artery
Answer: C
Explanation: The pterion is the thin junction of the frontal, parietal, temporal, and sphenoid bones overlying the anterior branch of the middle meningeal artery, which arises from the maxillary artery and enters through the foramen spinosum. Its rupture produces an epidural hematoma.

Q: An 80-year-old alcoholic man has progressive headache and confusion after a minor fall. CT shows a crescent-shaped collection between the dura and arachnoid. Which vessels are most likely torn?
A) Middle meningeal artery
B) Bridging (superior cerebral) veins
C) Anterior cerebral artery
D) Vertebral artery
E) Posterior communicating artery
Answer: B
Explanation: Subdural hematoma results from tearing of the bridging veins that cross the subdural space from the cerebral cortex to the superior sagittal sinus, particularly in elderly persons with brain atrophy. Epidural hematomas are usually arterial.

Q: A patient with cavernous sinus thrombosis cannot abduct the right eye. Which nerve is most vulnerable because it runs within the sinus next to the internal carotid artery rather than in its lateral wall?
A) Oculomotor nerve
B) Abducens nerve
C) Trochlear nerve
D) Ophthalmic nerve
E) Maxillary nerve
Answer: B
Explanation: The abducens nerve passes through the cavernous sinus lumen lateral to the internal carotid artery, whereas the oculomotor, trochlear, ophthalmic, and maxillary nerves lie in the lateral wall. Abducens paralysis causes medial deviation of the eye.

Q: A 45-year-old woman has a sudden severe headache, a dilated pupil, and a drooping eyelid with the eye turned down and out. Which aneurysm is the most likely cause?
A) Posterior communicating artery aneurysm compressing the oculomotor nerve
B) Anterior communicating artery aneurysm compressing the optic chiasm
C) Basilar tip aneurysm compressing the trochlear nerve
D) Middle cerebral artery aneurysm compressing the optic nerve
E) Vertebral artery aneurysm compressing the hypoglossal nerve
Answer: A
Explanation: The oculomotor nerve runs close to the posterior communicating artery as it passes from the midbrain; an aneurysm compresses its parasympathetic fibers (dilated pupil) and motor fibers, causing ptosis and a "down and out" eye.

Q: A child has hydrocephalus due to obstruction of arachnoid villi. Through which structures does cerebrospinal fluid normally enter the venous circulation?
A) Choroid plexus into the vein of Galen
B) Cerebral aqueduct into the sigmoid sinus
C) Arachnoid granulations into the superior sagittal sinus
D) Foramen of Monro into the cavernous sinus
E) Central canal into the internal jugular vein
Answer: C
Explanation: CSF, produced by the choroid plexuses, flows from the lateral ventricles through the interventricular foramen into the third ventricle, through the cerebral aqueduct to the fourth ventricle, out through its apertures to the subarachnoid space, and is absorbed by arachnoid granulations into the superior sagittal sinus.

Q: A skull base fracture damages structures passing through the jugular foramen. Which cranial nerves are at risk?
A) Facial, vestibulocochlear, and glossopharyngeal nerves
B) Glossopharyngeal, vagus, and accessory nerves
C) Oculomotor, trochlear, and abducens nerves
D) Hypoglossal and accessory nerves
E) Trigeminal, facial, and vagus nerves
Answer: B
Explanation: The jugular foramen transmits the glossopharyngeal, vagus, and accessory nerves and the internal jugular vein. The facial and vestibulocochlear nerves enter the internal acoustic meatus, and the hypoglossal nerve passes through the hypoglossal canal.

Q: A 30-year-old man has a fracture of the cribriform plate after a fall. He has clear fluid dripping from his nose and cannot smell. Which structures are injured?
A) Optic nerve and ophthalmic artery
B) Facial nerve and chorda tympani
C) Olfactory nerve fibers and meninges
D) Maxillary nerve and middle meningeal artery
E) Abducens nerve and internal carotid artery
Answer: C
Explanation: The cribriform plate of the ethmoid bone transmits the olfactory nerve filaments from the nasal mucosa to the olfactory bulb in the anterior cranial fossa; fracture causes anosmia and cerebrospinal fluid rhinorrhea when the meninges tear.

Q: Which structure traverses the optic canal together with the optic nerve?
A) Superior ophthalmic vein
B) Abducens nerve
C) Ophthalmic artery
D) Oculomotor nerve
E) Lacrimal nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The optic canal transmits the optic nerve and the ophthalmic artery. The superior orbital fissure transmits the oculomotor, trochlear, abducens, and ophthalmic nerve branches (lacrimal, frontal, nasociliary) and the ophthalmic veins.

Q: A patient with head trauma has diplopia on looking downward when the eye is adducted, and he tilts his head to compensate. Which muscle is paralyzed?
A) Superior oblique
B) Inferior oblique
C) Lateral rectus
D) Medial rectus
E) Superior rectus
Answer: A
Explanation: The trochlear nerve (CN IV) supplies the superior oblique muscle, which depresses the adducted eye (as in reading or descending stairs) and intorts it. The lateral rectus is supplied by the abducens nerve, and the other extraocular muscles by the oculomotor nerve.

Q: In a patient with complete oculomotor nerve palsy, which extraocular muscle will still function?
A) Lateral rectus
B) Medial rectus
C) Inferior rectus
D) Inferior oblique
E) Levator palpebrae superioris
Answer: A
Explanation: The lateral rectus (CN VI) and superior oblique (CN IV) are spared in oculomotor palsy, so the eye is abducted and slightly depressed ("down and out"). The medial, superior, and inferior recti, inferior oblique, and levator palpebrae superioris are paralyzed.

Q: A 60-year-old smoker has an apical lung tumor with ptosis, miosis, and facial anhidrosis. Which muscle is responsible for the ptosis in Horner syndrome?
A) Levator palpebrae superioris
B) Superior tarsal muscle
C) Orbicularis oculi
D) Frontalis
E) Dilator pupillae
Answer: B
Explanation: Interruption of the cervical sympathetic pathway paralyzes the superior tarsal (Müller) muscle, which elevates the upper eyelid slightly, causing mild ptosis; paralysis of the dilator pupillae causes miosis. The levator palpebrae superioris is supplied by the oculomotor nerve.

Q: Shining a light into one eye causes constriction of both pupils. Which structures carry the efferent limb of this reflex?
A) Optic nerve, lateral geniculate body, and optic radiation
B) Superior cervical ganglion and long ciliary nerves
C) Edinger-Westphal nucleus, oculomotor nerve, and ciliary ganglion
D) Facial nerve and pterygopalatine ganglion
E) Trigeminal nerve and ciliary ganglion
Answer: C
Explanation: The afferent limb is the optic nerve; the efferent limb is the parasympathetic fibers from the Edinger-Westphal nucleus through the oculomotor nerve to the ciliary ganglion and short ciliary nerves to the sphincter pupillae.

Q: A child has epiphora (excessive tearing) due to blockage of the nasolacrimal duct. Where does this duct drain into the nasal cavity?
A) Superior nasal meatus
B) Inferior nasal meatus
C) Middle nasal meatus
D) Sphenoethmoidal recess
E) Nasopharynx
Answer: B
Explanation: The nasolacrimal duct drains tears from the lacrimal sac into the inferior nasal meatus, below the inferior concha. The maxillary sinus and frontal sinus drain into the middle meatus.

Q: A patient with a lesion of the greater petrosal nerve has dry eyes. In which ganglion do the preganglionic fibers for the lacrimal gland synapse?
A) Ciliary ganglion
B) Otic ganglion
C) Submandibular ganglion
D) Superior cervical ganglion
E) Pterygopalatine ganglion
Answer: E
Explanation: Preganglionic parasympathetic fibers of the facial nerve travel in the greater petrosal nerve and the nerve of the pterygoid canal to the pterygopalatine ganglion; postganglionic fibers reach the lacrimal gland via the zygomatic and lacrimal nerves.

Q: A 65-year-old woman has acute angle-closure glaucoma. Aqueous humor normally leaves the anterior chamber through which structure?
A) Scleral venous sinus (canal of Schlemm)
B) Hyaloid canal
C) Central retinal vein
D) Vortex veins
E) Optic disc
Answer: A
Explanation: Aqueous humor is produced by the ciliary processes, flows from the posterior to the anterior chamber, and drains through the trabecular meshwork into the scleral venous sinus. Blockage increases intraocular pressure and damages the optic nerve.

Q: A 45-year-old woman begins to need reading glasses. During accommodation for near vision, which of the following changes normally occurs?
A) Ciliary muscle relaxes, tightening the suspensory ligament, and the lens becomes flatter
B) Dilator pupillae contracts and the lens becomes thinner
C) Ciliary muscle contracts, tightening the suspensory ligament, and the lens becomes flat
D) Ciliary muscle contracts, relaxing the suspensory ligament, and the lens becomes more convex
E) Superior tarsal muscle contracts and the lens becomes thicker
Answer: D
Explanation: For near vision, parasympathetic fibers (oculomotor nerve through the ciliary ganglion) contract the ciliary muscle, which relaxes tension on the suspensory ligament and allows the elastic lens to become thicker and more convex, along with pupillary constriction and convergence.

Q: A 25-year-old man has a dislocated temporomandibular joint after yawning. Which muscle is the principal one that depresses and protrudes the mandible to open the mouth?
A) Medial pterygoid
B) Lateral pterygoid
C) Masseter
D) Temporalis
E) Buccinator
Answer: B
Explanation: The lateral pterygoid pulls the condyle and articular disc forward and opens the mouth (with the suprahyoid muscles); all other muscles of mastication elevate the mandible. All are innervated by the mandibular nerve.

Q: A dentist anesthetizes the inferior alveolar nerve. Which nerve joining the lingual nerve carries taste from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue and preganglionic parasympathetic fibers to the submandibular ganglion?
A) Greater petrosal nerve
B) Lesser petrosal nerve
C) Auriculotemporal nerve
D) Chorda tympani
E) Glossopharyngeal nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The chorda tympani, a branch of the facial nerve, joins the lingual nerve in the infratemporal fossa, carrying taste fibers from the anterior two-thirds of the tongue and preganglionic parasympathetic fibers to the submandibular and sublingual glands via the submandibular ganglion.

Q: A 55-year-old man has a lesion of the hypoglossal nerve. Which tongue muscle will still function because it is not innervated by this nerve?
A) Genioglossus
B) Palatoglossus
C) Hyoglossus
D) Styloglossus
E) Superior longitudinal muscle
Answer: B
Explanation: All intrinsic and extrinsic muscles of the tongue are supplied by the hypoglossal nerve except the palatoglossus, which is innervated by the vagus nerve through the pharyngeal plexus.

Q: A 50-year-old man has a stone in the submandibular duct and undergoes surgery. Which nerve crosses the duct as it passes beneath it and is at risk?
A) Hypoglossal nerve
B) Glossopharyngeal nerve
C) Lingual nerve
D) Chorda tympani
E) Mylohyoid nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: In the floor of the mouth, the lingual nerve loops under the submandibular duct from lateral to medial, so it may be injured in surgery on the duct or gland. The hypoglossal nerve lies lateral and deep to the hyoglossus muscle.

Q: A 60-year-old man has a lesion of the right vagus nerve. When he says "ah," in which direction does the uvula deviate?
A) To the right, toward the lesion
B) It does not deviate but is elevated
C) To the left, away from the lesion
D) Upward only
E) Downward
Answer: C
Explanation: The vagus supplies the levator veli palatini and uvular muscles (via the pharyngeal plexus); the uvula is pulled toward the normal side, away from the lesion. The tensor veli palatini is supplied by the mandibular nerve.

Q: A newborn has a cleft in the hard and soft palates posterior to the incisive foramen. Failure of which structures to fuse is responsible?
A) Median nasal prominences
B) Lateral palatine processes (palatine shelves)
C) Maxillary prominence and median nasal prominence
D) Intermaxillary segment and maxillary prominence
E) First and second pharyngeal arches
Answer: B
Explanation: Cleft palate, posterior to the incisive foramen, results from failure of the lateral palatine processes to fuse with each other and the nasal septum. Cleft lip results from failure of the maxillary prominence to fuse with the medial nasal prominence.

Q: A neurologist tests the gag reflex. Which nerve carries the afferent limb?
A) Vagus nerve
B) Trigeminal nerve
C) Glossopharyngeal nerve
D) Facial nerve
E) Hypoglossal nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: The afferent limb of the gag reflex is the glossopharyngeal nerve (sensation from the pharyngeal wall), and the efferent limb is the vagus nerve (pharyngeal muscle contraction).

Q: A 40-year-old man has a lesion of the glossopharyngeal nerve. Which pharyngeal muscle is most likely paralyzed?
A) Superior constrictor
B) Middle constrictor
C) Inferior constrictor
D) Palatopharyngeus
E) Stylopharyngeus
Answer: E
Explanation: The stylopharyngeus is the only pharyngeal muscle innervated by the glossopharyngeal nerve; all other pharyngeal muscles are supplied by the vagus nerve through the pharyngeal plexus. It elevates the pharynx during swallowing and speaking.

Q: A 6-year-old child has a tonsillectomy and later complains of ear pain. Which nerve in the tonsillar bed explains this referred pain?
A) Vagus nerve
B) Facial nerve
C) Mandibular nerve
D) Lesser occipital nerve
E) Glossopharyngeal nerve
Answer: E
Explanation: The glossopharyngeal nerve lies in the tonsillar bed and supplies the tonsil and the mucosa of the middle ear through the tympanic branch; pain from the tonsil is referred to the ear.

Q: A 5-year-old child has mouth breathing and recurrent otitis media. An enlarged lymphoid mass in the nasopharynx obstructs the opening of the auditory tube. Which structure is enlarged?
A) Palatine tonsil
B) Lingual tonsil
C) Tubal tonsil only
D) Retropharyngeal lymph nodes
E) Pharyngeal tonsil (adenoids)
Answer: E
Explanation: The pharyngeal tonsil in the roof of the nasopharynx may obstruct the posterior nasal apertures and the auditory tubes when enlarged, causing mouth breathing and middle ear infections. The palatine tonsil lies between the palatoglossal and palatopharyngeal arches.

Q: A 20-year-old man has recurrent anterior nosebleeds from Kiesselbach's area of the nasal septum. Which contributing artery is NOT a branch of the external carotid artery?
A) Sphenopalatine artery
B) Greater palatine artery
C) Superior labial artery
D) Facial artery
E) Anterior ethmoidal artery
Answer: E
Explanation: The anterior ethmoidal artery arises from the ophthalmic artery (internal carotid system), whereas the sphenopalatine, greater palatine, and superior labial arteries come from the maxillary and facial branches of the external carotid. These vessels anastomose on the anterior septum.

Q: A 30-year-old woman has maxillary sinusitis. Where does the maxillary sinus drain into the nasal cavity?
A) Inferior meatus
B) Superior meatus
C) Sphenoethmoidal recess
D) Nasopharynx
E) Middle meatus through the hiatus semilunaris
Answer: E
Explanation: The maxillary sinus opens into the middle meatus through the hiatus semilunaris; because the opening is high on the medial wall, drainage is poor in the upright position. The nasolacrimal duct opens into the inferior meatus.

Q: A neurosurgeon removes a pituitary adenoma through the nasal cavity and sphenoid sinus. Where does the sphenoid sinus normally drain?
A) Inferior meatus
B) Middle meatus
C) Superior meatus
D) Sphenoethmoidal recess
E) Hiatus semilunaris
Answer: D
Explanation: The sphenoid sinus opens into the sphenoethmoidal recess above the superior concha. The sphenoid sinus lies below the pituitary fossa, so the transsphenoidal route provides access to the pituitary gland.

Q: Nerves and vessels pass from the pterygopalatine fossa to the nasal cavity through which opening?
A) Foramen rotundum
B) Pterygomaxillary fissure
C) Inferior orbital fissure
D) Greater palatine canal
E) Sphenopalatine foramen
Answer: E
Explanation: The sphenopalatine foramen connects the pterygopalatine fossa to the nasal cavity and transmits the sphenopalatine artery and nasopalatine and posterior nasal nerves. Foramen rotundum leads to the middle cranial fossa, and the pterygomaxillary fissure leads to the infratemporal fossa.

Q: A patient with bilateral paralysis of one intrinsic laryngeal muscle has stridor because the vocal folds cannot open. Which muscle is the only abductor of the vocal folds?
A) Lateral cricoarytenoid
B) Transverse arytenoid
C) Posterior cricoarytenoid
D) Cricothyroid
E) Thyroarytenoid
Answer: C
Explanation: The posterior cricoarytenoid rotates the arytenoid cartilages laterally, abducting the vocal folds and widening the rima glottidis. The lateral cricoarytenoid adducts, and the cricothyroid tenses the folds. All except the cricothyroid are supplied by the recurrent laryngeal nerve.

Q: A patient with complete upper airway obstruction requires an emergency airway. Through which structure is an emergency cricothyrotomy performed?
A) Thyrohyoid membrane
B) Quadrangular membrane
C) Between the second and third tracheal rings
D) Cricothyroid membrane
E) Vestibular fold
Answer: D
Explanation: The cricothyroid membrane (conus elasticus) between the thyroid and cricoid cartilages is superficial and avascular and lies below the vocal folds. A tracheostomy goes through tracheal rings, usually the second to fourth.

Q: Which part of the laryngeal cavity is the narrowest in the adult?
A) Laryngeal vestibule
B) Laryngeal ventricle
C) Rima glottidis
D) Infraglottic cavity
E) Rima vestibuli
Answer: C
Explanation: The rima glottidis, between the vocal folds, is the narrowest part of the laryngeal cavity in adults; it is bordered by the vocal folds and arytenoid cartilages. In children, the subglottic region is narrowest.

Q: During otoscopic examination of the right ear, a patient coughs reflexively. Which nerve supplies the external surface of the tympanic membrane and mediates this reflex?
A) Glossopharyngeal nerve only
B) Auricular branch of the vagus nerve
C) Chorda tympani
D) Great auricular nerve only
E) Facial nerve proper
Answer: B
Explanation: The external surface of the tympanic membrane is supplied by the auriculotemporal nerve and the auricular branch of the vagus nerve; stimulation of the latter can cause cough. The internal surface is supplied by the glossopharyngeal nerve.

Q: A child with acute otitis media develops mastoiditis. Through which structure does infection pass from the middle ear to the mastoid air cells?
A) Tegmen tympani
B) Aditus to the mastoid antrum
C) Eustachian tube
D) Oval window
E) Round window
Answer: B
Explanation: The middle ear communicates posteriorly with the mastoid antrum and air cells through the aditus to the mastoid antrum. The tegmen tympani is the thin roof separating the middle ear from the middle cranial fossa, a route for spread of infection to the meninges.

Q: A 55-year-old woman has progressive unilateral hearing loss and tinnitus from a tumor at the internal acoustic meatus. Which nerve is most likely also affected because it travels with the vestibulocochlear nerve?
A) Glossopharyngeal nerve
B) Trigeminal nerve
C) Vagus nerve
D) Facial nerve
E) Abducens nerve
Answer: D
Explanation: The facial and vestibulocochlear nerves enter the internal acoustic meatus together, so a vestibular schwannoma (acoustic neuroma) may produce facial weakness along with hearing loss, tinnitus, and imbalance.

Q: A 4-year-old has frequent middle ear infections. During swallowing, which muscle opens the auditory (pharyngotympanic) tube to equalize middle ear pressure?
A) Tensor tympani
B) Stapedius
C) Tensor veli palatini
D) Levator veli palatini only
E) Palatopharyngeus
Answer: C
Explanation: The tensor veli palatini (mandibular nerve) opens the cartilaginous part of the auditory tube during swallowing and yawning. The tensor tympani and stapedius are middle ear muscles that dampen ossicle movement.

Q: A 22-year-old man presented to his family physician with a laceration of the fibrous sheets or bands that cover his body under the skin and invest the muscles. Which of the following structures would most likely be injured?
A) Tendon
B) Fascia
C) Synovial tendon sheath
D) Aponeurosis
E) Ligament
Answer: B
Explanation: The fascia is a fibrous sheet or band that covers the body under the skin and invests the muscles. Although they are fibrous, tendons connect muscles to bones or cartilage, aponeuroses serve as the means of origin or insertion of a flat muscle, and ligaments connect bones to bones or cartilage. Synovial tendon sheets are tubular sacs filled with synovial fluid that wrap around the tendons.

Q: On the basis of the examination at her doctor’s office, a patient is told that her parasympathetic nerves are damaged. Which of the following muscles would most likely be affected?
A) Muscles in the hair follicles
B) Muscles in blood vessels
C) Muscles that act at the elbow joint
D) Muscles in the gastrointestinal (GI) tract
E) Muscles enclosed by epimysium
Answer: D
Explanation: Smooth muscles in the gastrointestinal tract are innervated by both parasympathetic and sympathetic nerves. Smooth muscles in the wall of the blood vessels and arrector pili muscles in hair follicles are innervated only by sympathetic nerves. Muscles that act at the elbow joint and muscles enclosed by epimysium are skeletal muscles that are innervated by somatic motor (general somatic efferent [GSE]) nerves.

Q: A 46-year-old male patient with high blood pressure was examined in the emergency department, and his physician found a leakage of blood from the blood vessel that normally carries richly oxygenated blood. Which of the following vessels would most likely be damaged?
A) Superior vena cava
B) Pulmonary arteries
C) Pulmonary veins
D) Portal vein
E) Coronary sinus
Answer: C
Explanation: Pulmonary veins return oxygenated blood to the heart from the lungs. Pulmonary arteries carry deoxygenated blood from the heart to the lungs for oxygen renewal. The portal vein carries deoxygenated blood with nutrients from the intestine to the liver. The superior vena cava and coronary sinus carry deoxygenated blood to the right atrium.

Q: A 16-year-old patient received a stab wound, and axons of the general somatic efferent (GSE) neurons to the shoulder muscles were severed. The damaged axons:
A) Would carry impulses toward the cell bodies
B) Would carry impulses away from the cell bodies mplete statements in this section is followed Select the one lettered answer or completion
C) Would carry pain impulses
D) Are several in numbers for multipolar neurons
E) Are found primarily in the gray matter
Answer: B
Explanation: The axons of the neurons carry impulses away from the cell bodies, and dendrites carry impulses to the cell bodies. The axons contain sensory or motor fibers. Multipolar neurons have several dendrites and one axon. The GSE neurons do not carry sensory impulses. The gray matter of the central nervous system consists largely of neuron cell bodies, dendrites, and neuroglia, whereas the white matter consists largely of axons and neuroglia

Q: A 16-year-old patient received a laceration of the posterior intercostal nerves by a penetrated knife blade. A pathologist obtained needle biopsy tissues and observed numerous degenerated cell bodies of the unipolar or pseudounipolar neurons. Which of the following structures would most likely provide the abnormal cell morphology?
A) Ventral horn of the spinal cord
B) Lateral horn of the spinal cord
C) Dorsal horn of the spinal cord
D) Dorsal root ganglion
E) Sympathetic chain ganglion
Answer: D
Explanation: Ventral, lateral, and dorsal horns and sympathetic chain ganglia contain multipolar neurons, whereas the dorsal root ganglion contains unipolar or pseudounipolar neurons. A laceration of the intercostal nerve injures GSE, postganglionic sympathetic general visceral efferent (GVE), general visceral afferent, and general somatic afferent (GSA) fibers, whose cell bodies are located in the anterior horn, sympathetic chain ganglia, and dorsal root ganglia.

Q: A 19-year-old college student came to his doctor’s office for a neurologic examination. His physician told him that normally synapses are absent in or on which of the following structures?
A) Anterior horn of the spinal cord
B) Dorsal root ganglia
C) Sympathetic chain ganglia
D) Dendrites
E) Cell bodies
Answer: B
Explanation: Dorsal root ganglia consist of cell bodies of the unipolar or pseudounipolar neurons and have no synapses. Axosomatic and axodendritic synapses are the most common, but axoaxonal and dendrodendritic contacts are also found in many nerve tissues.

Q: A 27-year-old woman involved in a car accident is brought into the emergency department. Her magnetic resonance imaging reveals that she has a laceration of the spinal cord at the L4 spinal cord level. Which of the following structures would you expect to be intact?
A) Dorsal horn
B) Lateral horn
C) Ventral horn
D) Gray matter
E) White matter
Answer: B
Explanation: The lateral horns are found in the gray matter of the spinal cord between T1 and L2 and also between S2 and S4. Therefore, the lateral horns are absent at the L4 spinal cord level.

Q: A 33-year-old male patient complains of feeling severe pain when he tries to turn his neck. A physician realizes that the problem is in his pivot (trochoid) joint. Which of the following joints would most likely be examined?
A) Atlantooccipital joint
B) Atlantoaxial joint
C) Carpometacarpal joint
D) Proximal tibiofibular joint
E) Intervertebral disks
Answer: B
Explanation: The atlantoaxial joint is the pivot or trochoid joint. The atlantooccipital joints are the condyloid (ellipsoidal) joints, the carpometacarpal joint of the thumb is the saddle (sellar) joint, and the proximal tibiofibular joint is the plane (gliding) joint. The intervertebral disk is the secondary cartilaginous (symphysis) joint.

Q: A patient presents with a loss of sensation to the skin over the shoulder. Injury to which of the following nerve cells would most likely affect the conduction of sensory information to the central nervous system?
A) Multipolar neurons
B) Bipolar neurons
C) Unipolar or pseudounipolar neurons
D) Neurons in the ventral horn
E) Neurons in sympathetic chain ganglia
Answer: C
Explanation: Sensation from the skin is carried by GSA fibers, and their cells are unipolar or pseudounipolar types located in the dorsal root ganglia. Multipolar neurons and neurons in the ventral horn and in sympathetic chain ganglia are motor neurons. Bipolar neurons are sensory neurons, but they are not somatic sensory neurons.

Q: A 7-year-old girl comes to the emergency department with severe diarrhea. Tests show that the diarrhea is due to decreased capacity of normal absorption in one of her organs. Which of the following organs is involved?
A) Stomach
B) Gallbladder
C) Large intestine
D) Liver
E) Pancreas
Answer: C
Explanation: The large intestine absorbs water, salts, and electrolytes. Hence, the patient’s diarrhea stems from an absorption problem. The stomach mixes food with mucus and gastric juice, which contains hydrochloric acid and enzymes, and forms chyme. The gallbladder receives bile, concentrates it, and stores it. The liver produces bile, whereas the pancreas secretes pancreatic juice, which contains digestive enzymes and which releases hormones, such as insulin and glucagon.

Q: A 16-year-old girl with urinary diseases comes to a local hospital. Her urologist’s examination and laboratory test results reveal that she has difficulty in removing wastes from the blood and in producing urine. Which of the following organs may have abnormal functions?
A) Ureter
B) Spleen
C) Urethra
D) Bladder
E) Kidney
Answer: E
Explanation: The urinary system includes the kidneys, which remove wastes from the blood and produce the urine; the ureters, which carry urine; the urinary bladder, which stores urine; and the urethra, which conveys urine from the bladder to the exterior of the body. The spleen filters blood to remove particulate matter and cellular residue, stores red blood cells, and produces lymphocytes. Because the patient is not producing urine properly, the malfunctioning organs are the kidneys.

Q: A 53-year-old man with a known history of emphysema is examined in the emergency department. Laboratory findings along with examination indicate that the patient is unable to exchange oxygen in the air and carbon dioxide in the blood. This exchange occurs in which portion of the respiratory system?
A) Bronchi
B) Alveolar (air) sac
C) Nasal cavity
D) Larynx
E) Trachea
Answer: B
Explanation: The respiratory portion of the lung contains the alveolar (air) sacs or alveoli, which are surrounded by networks of pulmonary capillaries. Oxygen and carbon dioxide exchange occurs across the thin walls of the alveoli and blood capillaries with the aid of the diaphragm and thoracic cage. The nasal cavity, larynx, trachea, and bronchi are air-conducting portions.

Q: A 26-year-old woman has an amenorrhea, followed by uterine bleeding, pelvic pain, and pelvic mass. Her obstetrician performed a thorough examination, and the patient was diagnosed as having an ectopic pregnancy. Which of the following organs is most likely to provide a normal site of fertilization?
A) Fundus of the uterus
B) Ampulla of the uterine tube
C) Fimbriae
D) Infundibulum of the uterine tube
E) Body of the uterus
Answer: B
Explanation: Fertilization occurs in the ampulla of the uterine tube, and a fertilized oocyte forms a blastocyst by day 7 after fertilization and becomes embedded or implanted in the wall of the uterus during the progestational (secretory) phase of the menstrual cycle. Fertilization is the process beginning with the penetration of the secondary oocyte by the sperm and completed by fusion of the male and female pronuclei.

Q: A 29-year-old woman with abdominal pain was admitted to a local hospital, and examination shows that a retroperitoneal infection is affecting a purely endocrine gland. Which of the following structures is infected?
A) Ovary
B) Suprarenal gland
C) Pancreas
D) Liver
E) Stomach
Answer: B
Explanation: The suprarenal gland is a retroperitoneal organ and is a purely endocrine gland. The pancreas is a retroperitoneal organ and contains endocrine cells, but it is not a purely endocrine gland. The liver and stomach contain endocrine cells, but they are not purely endocrine glands and also are surrounded by peritoneum. The ovary contains endocrine cells and is located in the pelvic cavity.

Q: A 36-year-old woman received a firstdegree burn on her neck, arm, and forearm from a house fire. Which of the following skin structures or functions is most likely damaged or impaired?
A) GSE nerves
B) Parasympathetic general visceral efferent nerves
C) Trophic hormone production
D) Exocrine gland secretion
E) Vitamin A production
Answer: D
Explanation: Skin has sweat glands and sebaceous glands, which are exocrine glands. Skin produces vitamin D, but it does not produce a trophic hormone and does not produce vitamin A. In addition, skin contains no GSE and parasympathetic GVE nerve fibers.

Q: A 21-year-old patient has a lesion of the upper trunk of the brachial plexus (Erb– Duchenne paralysis). Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?
A) Paralysis of the rhomboid major
B) Inability to elevate the arm above the horizontal
C) Arm tending to lie in medial rotation
D) Loss of sensation on the medial side of the arm
E) Inability to adduct the thumb
Answer: C
Explanation: A lesion of the upper trunk of the brachial plexus results in a condition called “waiter’s tip hand,” in which the arm tends to lie in medial rotation because of paralysis of lateral rotators and abductors of the arm. The long thoracic nerve, which arises from the root (C5–C7) of the brachial plexus, innervates the serratus anterior muscle that can elevate the arm above the horizontal. The dorsal scapular nerve, which arises from the root (C5), innervates the rhomboid major. The medial side of the arm receives cutaneous innervation from the medial brachial cutaneous nerve of the medial cord. The adductor pollicis is innervated by the ulnar nerve.

Q: A patient comes in with a gunshot wound and requires surgery in which his thoracoacromial trunk needs to be ligated. Which of the following arterial branches would maintain normal blood flow?
A) Acromial
B) Pectoral
C) Clavicular
D) Deltoid
E) Superior thoracic
Answer: E
Explanation: The superior thoracic artery is a direct branch of the axillary artery. The thoracoacromial trunk has four branches: the pectoral, clavicular, acromial, and deltoid.

Q: A 29-year-old man comes in with a stab wound, cannot raise his arm above horizontal, and exhibits a condition known as “winged scapula.” Which of the following structures of the brachial plexus would most likely be damaged?
A) Medial cord
B) Posterior cord
C) Lower trunk
D) Roots
E) Upper trunk
Answer: D
Explanation: Winged scapula is caused by paralysis of the serratus anterior muscle that results from damage to the long thoracic nerve, which arises from the roots of the brachial plexus (C5–C7).

Q: A 16-year-old patient has weakness flexing the metacarpophalangeal joint of the ring finger and is unable to adduct the same finger. Which of the following muscles is most likely paralyzed?
A) Flexor digitorum profundus
B) Extensor digitorum
C) Lumbrical omplete statements in this section is followed Select the one lettered answer or completion
D) Dorsal interosseous
E) Palmar interosseous
Answer: E
Explanation: The dorsal and palmar interosseous and lumbrical muscles can flex the metacarpophalangeal joints and extend the interphalangeal joints. The palmar interosseous muscles adduct the fingers, while the dorsal interosseous muscles abduct the fingers. The flexor digitorum profundus flexes the distal interphalangeal (DIP) joints.

Q: A 27-year-old patient presents with an inability to draw the scapula forward and downward because of paralysis of the pectoralis minor. Which of the following would most likely be a cause of his condition?
A) Fracture of the clavicle
B) Injury to the posterior cord of the brachial plexus
C) Fracture of the coracoid process
D) Axillary nerve injury
E) Defects in the posterior wall of the axilla
Answer: C
Explanation: The pectoralis minor inserts on the coracoid process, originates from the second to the fifth ribs, and is innervated by the medial and lateral pectoral nerves that arise from the medial and lateral cords of the brachial plexus. It depresses the shoulder and forms the anterior wall of the axilla. The pectoralis minor has no attachment on the clavicle.

Q: A 22-year-old patient received a stab wound in the chest that injured the intercostobrachial nerve. Which of the following conditions results from the described lesion of the nerve?
A) Inability to move the ribs
B) Loss of tactile sensation on the lateral aspect of the arm
C) Absence of sweating on the posterior aspect of the arm
D) Loss of sensory fibers from the second intercostal nerve
E) Damage to the sympathetic preganglionic fibers
Answer: D
Explanation: The intercostobrachial nerve arises from the lateral cutaneous branch of the second intercostal nerve and pierces the intercostal and serratus anterior muscles. It may communicate with the medial brachial cutaneous nerve, and it supplies skin on the medial side of the arm. It contains no skeletal motor fibers but does contain sympathetic postganglionic fibers, which supply sweat glands.

Q: A 16 year-old boy fell from a motorcycle, and his radial nerve was severely damaged because of a fracture of the midshaft of the humerus. Which of the following conditions would most likely result from this accident?
A) Loss of wrist extension leading to wrist drop
B) Weakness in pronating the forearm
C) Sensory loss over the ventral aspect of the base of the thumb
D) Inability to oppose the thumb
E) Inability to abduct the fingers
Answer: A
Explanation: Injury to the radial nerve results in loss of wrist extension, leading to wrist drop. The median nerve innervates the pronator teres, pronator quadratus, and opponens pollicis muscles and the skin over the ventral aspect of the thumb. The ulnar nerve innervates the dorsal interosseous muscles, which act to abduct the fingers.

Q: A patient comes in complaining that she cannot flex her proximal interphalangeal joints. Which of the following muscles appear(s) to be paralyzed on further examination of her finger?
A) Palmar interossei
B) Dorsal interossei
C) Flexor digitorum profundus
D) Flexor digitorum superficialis
E) Lumbricals
Answer: D
Explanation: The flexor digitorum superficialis muscle flexes the proximal interphalangeal joints. The flexor digitorum profundus muscle flexes the DIP joints. The palmar and dorsal interossei and lumbricals can flex metacarpophalangeal joints and extend the interphalangeal joints. The palmar interossei adduct the fingers, and the dorsal interossei abduct the fingers.

Q: A 21-year-old woman walks in with a shoulder and arm injury after falling during horseback riding. Examination indicates that she cannot adduct her arm because of paralysis of which of the following muscles?
A) Teres minor
B) Supraspinatus
C) Latissimus dorsi
D) Infraspinatus
E) Serratus anterior
Answer: C
Explanation: The latissimus dorsi adducts the arm, and the supraspinatus muscle abducts the arm. The infraspinatus and the teres minor rotate the arm laterally. The serratus anterior rotates the glenoid cavity of the scapula upward, abducts the arm, and elevates it above a horizontal position.

Q: A 35-year-old man walks in with a stab wound to the most medial side of the proximal portion of the cubital fossa. Which of the following structures would most likely be damaged?
A) Biceps brachii tendon
B) Radial nerve
C) Brachial artery
D) Radial recurrent artery
E) Median nerve
Answer: E
Explanation: The contents of the cubital fossa from medial to lateral side are the median nerve, the brachial artery, the biceps brachii tendon, and the radial nerve. Thus, the median nerve is damaged. The radial recurrent artery ascends medial to the radial nerve.

Q: The police bring in a murder suspect who has been in a gunfight with a police officer. The suspect was struck by a bullet in the arm; his median nerve has been damaged. Which of the following symptoms is likely produced by this nerve damage?
A) Waiter’s tip hand
B) Claw hand
C) Wrist drop
D) Ape hand
E) Flattening of the hypothenar eminence
Answer: D
Explanation: Injury to the median nerve produces the ape hand (a hand with the thumb permanently extended). Injury to the radial nerve results in loss of wrist extension, leading to wrist drop. Damage to the upper trunk of the brachial plexus produces waiter’s tip hand. A claw hand and flattening of the hypothenar eminence or atrophy of the hypothenar muscles result from damage to the ulnar nerve.

Q: An automobile body shop worker has his middle finger crushed while working on a transmission. Which of the following muscles is most likely to retain function?
A) Extensor digitorum
B) Flexor digitorum profundus
C) Palmar interosseous
D) Dorsal interosseous
E) Lumbrical
Answer: C
Explanation: The extensor digitorum, flexor digitorum profundus, dorsal interosseous, and lumbrical muscles are attached to the middle digit, but no palmar interosseous muscle is attached to the middle digit.

Q: A 14-year-old boy falls on his outstretched hand and has a fracture of the scaphoid bone. The fracture is most likely accompanied by a rupture of which of the following arteries?
A) Brachial artery
B) Ulnar artery
C) Deep palmar arterial arch
D) Radial artery
E) Princeps pollicis artery
Answer: D
Explanation: The scaphoid bone forms the floor of the anatomic snuffbox, through which the radial artery passes to enter the palm. The radial artery divides into the princeps pollicis artery and the deep palmar arch.

Q: A 12-year-old boy walks in; he fell out of a tree and fractured the upper portion of his humerus. Which of the following nerves are intimately related to the humerus and are most likely to be injured by such a fracture?
A) Axillary and musculocutaneous
B) Radial and ulnar
C) Radial and axillary
D) Median and musculocutaneous
E) Median and ulnar
Answer: C
Explanation: The axillary nerve passes posteriorly around the surgical neck of the humerus, and the radial nerve lies in the radial groove of the middle of the shaft of the humerus. The ulnar nerve passes behind the medial epicondyle, and the median nerve is vulnerable to injury by supracondylar fracture of the humerus, but these nerves lie close to or in contact with the lower portion of the humerus. The musculocutaneous nerve is not in direct contact with the humerus.

Q: A man injures his wrist on broken glass. Which of the following structures entering the palm superficial to the flexor retinaculum may be damaged?
A) Ulnar nerve and median nerve
B) Median nerve and flexor digitorum profundus
C) Median nerve and flexor pollicis longus
D) Ulnar artery and ulnar nerve
E) Ulnar nerve and flexor digitorum superficialis
Answer: D
Explanation: Structures entering the palm superficial to the flexor retinaculum include the ulnar nerve, ulnar artery, palmaris longus tendon, and palmar cutaneous branch of the median nerve. The median nerve, the flexor pollicis longus, and the flexor digitorum superficialis and profundus run deep to the flexor retinaculum.

Q: A patient with Bennett’s fracture (a fracture of the base of the first metacarpal bone) experiences an impaired thumb movement. Which of the following intrinsic muscles of the thumb is most likely injured?
A) Abductor pollicis brevis
B) Flexor pollicis brevis (superficial head)
C) Opponens pollicis
D) Adductor pollicis
E) Flexor pollicis brevis (deep head)
Answer: C
Explanation: The opponens pollicis inserts on the first metacarpal. All other intrinsic muscles of the thumb, including the abductor pollicis brevis, the flexor pollicis brevis, and the adductor pollicis muscles, insert on the proximal phalanges.

Q: A 27-year-old pianist with a known carpal tunnel syndrome experiences difficulty in finger movements. Which of the following intrinsic muscles of her hand is paralyzed?
A) Palmar interossei and adductor pollicis
B) Dorsal interossei and lateral two lumbricals
C) Lateral two lumbricals and opponens pollicis
D) Abductor pollicis brevis and palmar interossei
E) Medial two and lateral two lumbricals
Answer: C
Explanation: The median nerve innervates the abductor pollicis brevis, opponens pollicis, and two lateral lumbricals. The ulnar nerve innervates all interossei (palmar and dorsal), the adductor pollicis, and the two medial lumbricals.

Q: A 31-year-old roofer walks in with tenosynovitis resulting from a deep penetrated wound in the palm by a big nail. Examination indicates that he has an infection in the ulnar bursa. This infection most likely resulted in necrosis of which of the following tendons?
A) Tendon of the flexor carpi ulnaris
B) Tendon of the flexor pollicis longus
C) Tendon of the flexor digitorum profundus
D) Tendon of the flexor carpi radialis
E) Tendon of the palmaris longus
Answer: C
Explanation: The ulnar bursa, or common synovial flexor sheath, contains the tendons of both the flexor digitorum superficialis and profundus muscles. The radial bursa envelops the tendon of the flexor pollicis longus. The tendons of the flexor carpi ulnaris and the palmaris longus are not contained in the ulnar bursa.

Q: An 18-year-old boy involved in an automobile accident presents with arm that cannot abduct. His paralysis is caused by damage to which of the following nerves?
A) Suprascapular and axillary
B) Thoracodorsal and upper subscapular
C) Axillary and musculocutaneous
D) Radial and lower subscapular
E) Suprascapular and dorsal scapular
Answer: A
Explanation: The abductors of the arm are the deltoid and supraspinatus muscles, which are innervated by the axillary and suprascapular nerves, respectively. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies the latissimus dorsi, which can adduct, extend, and rotate the arm medially. The upper and lower subscapular nerves supply the subscapularis, and the lower subscapular nerve also supplies the teres major; both of these structures can adduct and rotate the arm medially. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the flexors of the arm, and the radial nerve supplies the extensors of the arm. The dorsal scapular nerve supplies the levator scapulae and rhomboid muscles; these muscles elevate and adduct the scapula, respectively.

Q: A 17-year-old boy with a stab wound received multiple injuries on the upper part of the arm and required surgery. If the brachial artery were ligated at its origin, which of the following arteries would supply blood to the profunda brachii artery?
A) Lateral thoracic
B) Subscapular
C) Posterior humeral circumflex
D) Superior ulnar collateral
E) Radial recurrent
Answer: C
Explanation: The posterior humeral circumflex artery anastomoses with an ascending branch of the profunda brachii artery, whereas the lateral thoracic and subscapular arteries do not. The superior ulnar collateral and radial recurrent arteries arise inferior to the origin of the profunda brachii artery.

Q: A 23-year-old woman who receives a deep cut to her ring finger by a kitchen knife is unable to move the metacarpophalangeal joint. Which of the following pairs of nerves was damaged?
A) Median and ulnar
B) Radial and median
C) Musculocutaneous and ulnar
D) Ulnar and radial
E) Radial and axillary
Answer: D
Explanation: The metacarpophalangeal joint of the ring finger is flexed by the lumbrical, palmar, and dorsal interosseous muscles, which are innervated by the ulnar nerve. The extensor digitorum, which is innervated by the radial nerve, extends this joint. The musculocutaneous and axillary nerves do not supply muscles of the hand. The median nerve supplies the lateral two lumbricals, which can flex metacarpophalangeal joints of the index and middle fingers.

Q: A 27-year-old baseball player is hit on his forearm by a high-speed ball during the World Series, and the muscles that form the floor of the cubital fossa appear to be torn. Which of the following groups of muscles have lost their functions?
A) Brachioradialis and supinator
B) Brachialis and supinator
C) Pronator teres and supinator
D) Supinator and pronator quadratus
E) Brachialis and pronator teres
Answer: B
Explanation: The brachialis and supinator muscles form the floor of the cubital fossa. The brachioradialis and pronator teres muscles form the lateral and medial boundaries, respectively. The pronator quadratus is attached to the distal ends of the radius and the ulna.

Q: A 23-year-old man complains of numbness on the medial side of the arm following a stab wound in the axilla. On examination, he is diagnosed with an injury of his medial brachial cutaneous nerve. In which of the following structures are the cell bodies of the damaged nerve involved in numbness located?
A) Sympathetic chain ganglion
B) Dorsal root ganglion
C) Anterior horn of the spinal cord
D) Lateral horn of the spinal cord
E) Posterior horn of the spinal cord
Answer: B
Explanation: The medial brachial cutaneous nerve contains sensory (general somatic afferent [GSA]) fibers that have cell bodies in the dorsal root ganglia, and an injury of these GSA fibers causes numbness of the medial side of the arm. It also contains sympathetic postganglionic fibers that have cell bodies in the sympathetic chain ganglia. The anterior horn of the spinal cord contains cell bodies of skeletal motor (general somatic efferent [GSE]) fibers, and the lateral horn contains cell bodies of sympathetic preganglionic fibers. The posterior horn contains cell bodies of interneurons.

Q: A 38-year-old homebuilder was involved in an accident and is unable to supinate his forearm. Which of the following nerves are most likely damaged?
A) Suprascapular and axillary
B) Musculocutaneous and median
C) Axillary and radial
D) Radial and musculocutaneous
E) Median and ulnar
Answer: D
Explanation: The supinator and biceps brachii muscles, which are innervated by the radial and musculocutaneous nerves, respectively, produce supination of the forearm. This is a question of two muscles that can supinate the forearm.

Q: A 31-year-old patient complains of sensory loss over the anterior and posterior surfaces of the medial third of the hand and the medial one and one-half fingers. He is diagnosed by a physician as having “funny bone” symptoms. Which of the following nerves is injured?
A) Axillary
B) Radial
C) Median
D) Ulnar
E) Musculocutaneous
Answer: D
Explanation: The ulnar nerve supplies sensory fibers to the skin over the palmar and dorsal surfaces of the medial third of the hand and the medial one and one-half fingers. The median nerve innervates the skin of the lateral side of the palm; the palmar side of the lateral three and one-half fingers; and the dorsal side of the index finger, the middle finger, and one-half of the ring finger. The radial nerve innervates the skin of the radial side of the hand and the radial two and one-half digits over the proximal phalanx.

Q: A patient with a deep stab wound in the middle of the forearm has impaired movement of the thumb. Examination indicates a lesion of the anterior interosseous nerve. Which of the following muscles is paralyzed?
A) Flexor pollicis longus and brevis
B) Flexor pollicis longus and opponens pollicis
C) Flexor digitorum profundus and pronator quadratus
D) Flexor digitorum profundus and superficialis
E) Flexor pollicis brevis and pronator quadratus
Answer: C
Explanation: The anterior interosseous nerve is a branch of the median nerve and supplies the flexor pollicis longus, half of the flexor digitorum profundus, and the pronator quadratus. The median nerve supplies the pronator teres, flexor digitorum superficialis, palmaris longus, and flexor carpi radialis muscles. A muscular branch (the recurrent branch) of the median nerve innervates the thenar muscles.

Q: A 29-year-old patient comes in; he cannot flex the distal interphalangeal (DIP) joint of the index finger. His physician determines that he has nerve damage from a supracondylar fracture. Which of the following conditions is also a symptom of this nerve damage?
A) Inability to flex the DIP joint of the ring finger
B) Atrophy of the hypothenar eminence
C) Loss of sensation over the distal part of the second digit
D) Paralysis of all the thumb muscles
E) Loss of supination
Answer: C
Explanation: The flexor digitorum profundus muscle flexes the DIP joints of the index and middle fingers and is innervated by the median nerve, which also supplies sensation over the distal part of the second digit. The same muscle flexes the DIP joints of the ring and little fingers but receives innervation from the ulnar nerve, which also innervates the hypothenar muscles. The median nerve innervates the thenar muscles. The radial nerve innervates the supinator, abductor pollicis longus, and extensor pollicis longus and brevis muscles. The ulnar nerve innervates the adductor pollicis. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the biceps brachii that can supinate the arm.

Q: A 27-year-old man with cubital tunnel syndrome complains of numbness and tingling in the ring and little finger and back and sides of his hand because of damage to a nerve in the tunnel at the elbow. Which of the following muscles is most likely to be paralyzed?
A) Flexor digitorum superficialis
B) Opponens pollicis
C) Two medial lumbricals
D) Pronator teres
E) Supinator
Answer: C
Explanation: The ulnar nerve innervates the two medial lumbricals. However, the median nerve innervates the two lateral lumbricals, the flexor digitorum superficialis, the opponens pollicis, and the pronator teres muscles.

Q: A secretary comes in to your office complaining of pain in her wrists from typing all day. You determine that she likely has carpal tunnel syndrome. Which of the following conditions would help you determine the diagnosis?
A) Inability to adduct the little finger
B) Inability to flex the DIP joint of the ring finger
C) Flattened thenar eminence
D) Loss of skin sensation of the medial one and one-half fingers
E) Atrophied adductor pollicis muscle
Answer: C
Explanation: The carpal tunnel contains the median nerve and the tendons of flexor pollicis longus, flexor digitorum profundus, and flexor digitorum superficialis muscles. Carpal tunnel syndrome results from injury to the median nerve, which supplies the thenar muscle. Thus, injury to this nerve causes the flattened thenar eminence. The middle finger has no attachment for the adductors. The ulnar nerve innervates the medial half of the flexor digitorum profundus muscle, which allows flexion of the DIP joints of the ring and little fingers. The ulnar nerve supplies the skin over the medial one and one-half fingers and adductor pollicis muscle.

Q: A man is unable to hold typing paper between his index and middle fingers. Which of the following nerves was likely injured?
A) Radial nerve
B) Median nerve
C) Ulnar nerve
D) Musculocutaneous nerve
E) Axillary nerve
Answer: C
Explanation: To hold a typing paper, the index finger is adducted by the palmar interosseous muscle, and the middle finger is abducted by the dorsal interosseous muscle. Both muscles are innervated by the ulnar nerve.

Q: The victim of an automobile accident has a destructive injury of the proximal row of carpal bones. Which of the following bones is most likely damaged?
A) Capitate
B) Hamate
C) Trapezium
D) Triquetrum
E) Trapezoid
Answer: D
Explanation: The proximal row of carpal bones consists of the scaphoid, lunate, triquetrum, and pisiform bones, whereas the distal row consists of the trapezium, trapezoid, capitate, and hamate bones.

Q: A patient has a torn rotator cuff of the shoulder joint as the result of an automobile accident. Which of the following muscle tendons is intact and has normal function?
A) Supraspinatus
B) Subscapularis
C) Teres major
D) Teres minor
E) Infraspinatus
Answer: C
Explanation: The rotator cuff consists of the tendons of the supraspinatus, infraspinatus, subscapularis, and teres minor muscles. It stabilizes the shoulder joint by holding the head of the humerus in the glenoid cavity during movement. The teres major inserts on the medial lip of the intertubercular groove of the humerus.

Q: A patient complains of having pain with repeated movements of his thumb (claudication). His physician performs the Allen test and finds an insufficiency of the radial artery. Which of the following conditions would be a result of the radial artery stenosis?
A) A marked decrease in the blood flow in the superficial palmar arterial arch
B) Decreased pulsation in the artery passing superficial to the flexor retinaculum
C) Ischemia of the entire extensor muscles of the forearm
D) A marked decrease in the blood flow in the princeps pollicis artery
E) A low blood pressure in the anterior interosseous artery
Answer: D
Explanation: The radial artery divides into the princeps pollicis artery and the deep palmar arterial arch. Thus, stenosis of the radial artery results in a decreased blood flow in the princeps pollicis artery. The superficial palmar arterial arch is formed primarily by the ulnar artery, which passes superficial to the flexor retinaculum. The extensor compartment of the forearm receives blood from the posterior interosseous artery, which arises from the common interosseous branch of the ulnar artery. However, the radial and radial recurrent arteries supply the brachioradialis and the extensor carpi radialis longus and brevis.

Q: A patient bleeding from the shoulder secondary to a knife wound is in fair condition because there is vascular anastomosis around the shoulder. Which of the following arteries is most likely a direct branch of the subclavian artery that is involved in the anastomosis?
A) Dorsal scapular artery
B) Thoracoacromial artery
C) Circumflex scapular artery
D) Transverse cervical artery
E) Suprascapular artery
Answer: A
Explanation: The dorsal scapular artery arises directly from the third part of the subclavian artery and replaces the deep (descending) branch of the transverse cervical artery. The suprascapular and transverse cervical arteries are branches of the thyrocervical trunk of the subclavian artery. The thoracoacromial artery is a short trunk from the first or second part of the axillary artery and has pectoral, clavicular, acromial, and deltoid branches.

Q: During a breast examination of a 56-year-old woman, the physician found a palpable mass in her breast. Which of the following characteristics of breast cancer and its diagnosis is correct?
A) Elevated nipple
B) Polymastia
C) Shortening of the clavipectoral fascia
D) Dimpling of the overlying skin
E) Enlargement of the breast
Answer: D
Explanation: Breast cancer may cause dimpling of the overlying skin because of shortening of the suspensory (Cooper’s) ligaments and inverted or retracted nipple because of pulling on the lactiferous ducts. Polymastia is a condition in which more than two breasts are present.

Q: A patient with a stab wound receives a laceration of the musculocutaneous nerve. Which of the following conditions is most likely to have occurred?
A) Lack of sweating on the lateral side of the forearm
B) Inability to extend the forearm
C) Paralysis of brachioradialis muscle
D) Loss of tactile sensation on the arm
E) Constriction of blood vessels on the hand
Answer: A
Explanation: The musculocutaneous nerve contains sympathetic postganglionic fibers that supply sweat glands and blood vessels on the lateral side of the forearm as the lateral antebrachial cutaneous nerve. The musculocutaneous nerve does not supply the extensors of the forearm and the brachioradialis.
